Path-dependent processes from signatures
Eduardo Abi Jaber, Louis-Amand Gérard, Yuxing Huang

Abstract
We provide explicit series expansions to certain stochastic path-dependent integral equations in terms of the path signature of the time augmented driving Brownian motion. Our framework encompasses a large class of stochastic linear Volterra and delay equations and in particular the fractional Brownian motion with a Hurst index H ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) H\in(0,1) . Our expressions allow to disentangle an infinite dimensional Markovian structure and open the door to straightforward and simple approximation schemes, that we illustrate numerically. A key application is the derivation of explicit series representations for both conditional and unconditional moments.



Mathematics Subject Classification (2010):

60L10, 60L70, 60H20, 60G22

Keywords:

Path-signatures, Volterra processes, Stochastic Delay equations, fractional Brownian motion.

1
Introduction

We provide explicit solutions to certain stochastic path-dependent integral equations in the form

X_{t}=X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}b(t,s,(X_{u})_{{0\leq}u\leq s})\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(t,s,(X_{u})_{{0\leq}u\leq s})\mathrm{d}W_{s},

for some linear coefficients
b,\sigma
, in terms of the path signature of the time-augmented driving Brownian motion
(t,W_{t})_{t\geq 0}
.

We consider two non-Markovian specifications:

•

The Volterra case
, where the coefficients
b,\sigma
are of the form

g(t,s,(X_{u})_{{0\leq}u\leq s})=K(t-s)(a+bX_{s}),

for some locally integrable deterministic kernel
K
and
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. The Riemann-Liouville Fractional Brownian motion and Gaussian Volterra processes constitute a particular case.

•

The Delay case
, where the coefficients
b,\sigma
are of the form

g(t,s,(X_{u})_{{0\leq}u\leq s})=a+bX_{s}+\int_{0}^{s}K(s-u)X_{u}\mathrm{d}u.

In both cases, the unique strong solution
X
is a non-anticipative measurable function of the whole path of the driving Brownian motion, that is

X_{t}=f(t,(W_{u})_{{0\leq}u\leq t}),\quad t\geq 0,

for some measurable functional
f
.

Our main contribution is to explicit this functional
f
. More precisely, we show that the solution
X
can be written in terms of an infinite linear combination of the signature process
\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}
of the time extended Brownian motion
\widehat{W}_{t}:=(t,W_{t})
defined by the infinite sequence of iterated integrals in the Stratonovich sense:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\left(1,\begin{pmatrix}t\\
W_{t}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\frac{t^{2}}{2!}&\int_{0}^{t}s\mathrm{d}W_{s}\\
\int_{0}^{t}W_{s}\mathrm{d}s&\frac{W_{t}^{2}}{2!}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\frac{t^{3}}{3!}&\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}u\mathrm{d}W_{u}\mathrm{d}s&\\
\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}W_{u}\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}s&\frac{1}{2!}\int_{0}^{t}W_{s}^{2}\mathrm{d}s&\\
&\frac{1}{2!}\int_{0}^{t}s^{2}\mathrm{d}W_{s}&\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}u\mathrm{d}W_{u}\circ\mathrm{d}W_{s}\\
&\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}W_{u}du\circ\mathrm{d}W_{s}&\frac{W_{t}^{3}}{3!}\end{pmatrix},\cdots\right),

that is

\displaystyle X_{t}=\left\langle\bm{\ell}_{t},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle,\quad t\geq 0,

(1.1)

where the (possibly) time-dependent deterministic coefficients
\bm{\ell}_{t}
, given by a sequence of numbers indexed by words, are explicit.

To highlight our strategy, consider a generalized geometric Brownian motion process
X
solution to

\displaystyle X_{t}=x+\int_{0}^{t}(a+bX_{s})\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}(\alpha+\beta X_{s})\circ\mathrm{d}W_{s},\quad\text{with }x,a,b,\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R},

(1.2)

where
\circ
denotes Stratonovich integration rule.

Our recipe:

1.

Translate the probabilistic equation into an algebraic linear equation on
\bm{\ell}
:

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}=x{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}+(a+b\bm{\ell}){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+(\alpha+\beta\bm{\ell}){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},

(1.3)

where concatenation with the letters
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}
play respectively the role of integration with respect to
\mathrm{d}t
and
\mathrm{d}W_{t}
(in the sense of Stratonovich).

2.

Solve the linear algebraic equation (
1.3
) by writing

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}=\bm{p}+\bm{\ell}\bm{q},

(1.4)

with

\displaystyle\bm{p}=(x{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}+a{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+\alpha{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}})\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{q}=b{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+\beta{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},

(1.5)

to get

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}=\bm{p}{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{q}\right)}^{-1}=\bm{p}\sum_{n\geq 0}\bm{q}^{\otimes n}.

(1.6)

3.

Argue that the infinite series
\langle\bm{\ell},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\rangle
is well-defined and solves the stochastic differential equation (
1.2
).

The recipe is simple yet powerful, as it remains robust across non-Markovian settings and for multi-dimensional Brownian motions, with the same algebraic equation (
1.4
) but with different
\bm{p}
and
\bm{q}
. To make the analysis rigorous, we proceed as follows. First, we study the existence of solutions of the algebraic equation (
1.3
) taking values in the extended tensor algebra together with some estimates for the solutions, see Proposition
2.2
and Theorem
3.9
. Second, since
\bm{\ell}
can have infinitely many non-zero elements, a delicate analysis is needed to make sense of the series expansions in (
1.1
), this is the object of Section
3.2
. In particular, we derive a tractable criterion for the global convergence in the supremum norm which is new compared to the related literature on infinite series of signature elements, see for instance
Ben Arous [9]
,
Cuchiero, Svaluto-Ferro, and
Teichmann [17]
.
The third step, a central ingredient for the derivation of our representation formulas in Section
4
is an Itô’s formula for possibly infinite linear combinations of signature elements, see Theorem
3.3
. Finally, in Section
3.4
we derive a tractable criterion based on the domination by a shuffle exponential of
\bm{p}
and
\bm{q}
that validates the aforementioned steps, i.e. that the linear functional is well-defined, converges against the signature and is an Itô process.

We provide two types of representations: (i) time-independent representations (
1.1
) for non-Markovian stochastic Volterra and delay equations with smooth enough kernels
K
where the coefficients
\bm{\ell}
do not depend on time, see Theorems
4.2
and
4.4
. Such
\bm{\ell}
would in principal correspond to the coefficients of a Stochastic–Taylor expansion applied in a path-dependent setting, except that here we compute
\bm{\ell}
algebraically and establish the convergence of the Taylor series. For more general kernels, we provide approximation results in Corollary
4.3
; but also (ii) time-dependent representations (
1.1
) with
\bm{\ell}_{t}
depending on time which allowed us to recover more general Gaussian processes in Theorem
4.5
with singular kernels. For instance, for an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process, a time-dependent representation involves

\bm{\ell}_{t}^{\textnormal{OU}}=e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}-\kappa(t{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}})}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},

which is the algebraic translation of the probabilistic expression

X_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\kappa(t-s)}\mathrm{d}W_{s},

with
e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}}
the shuffle exponential (
2.10
).

The representation (
1.1
) has at least two main features. First, it allows to disentangle an infinite dimensional Markovian structure in terms of the process
\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}
. Second it opens the door to straightforward and simple approximation schemes by truncating
\bm{\ell}_{t}
, numerical illustrations are provided in Section
4.4
. A key application is the derivation of explicit series representations for both conditional and unconditional moments of Volterra and delay processes, which can be efficiently computed numerically without relying on Monte Carlo simulations, as shown in Section
5
. In particular, the linear Volterra process is a polynomial Volterra process in the terminology of
Abi Jaber et al. [5]
, where unconditional moments were derived in terms of deterministic integral equations, without any treatment of conditional moments or numerical methods. Our results provide an alternative explicit representation for both conditional and unconditional moments and, most importantly, offer the first numerical implementation for such process.

Motivation.
The elegant framework of path signatures, as introduced by
Chen [12]
and extensively used in rough path theory
[
25
,
36
]
, not only captures the intricate dynamics of path-dependent processes but also facilitates their integration into various fields of applications. A notable attribute driving the recent prominence of signatures is their inherent linearization property, or equivalently the universal approximation property of linear functionals of the signature, and the fact that signatures characterize paths, see
[
15
,
27
]
. These properties have found many practical applications in Machine Learning (
Chevyrev and Kormilitzin [14]
,
Fermanian [22]
,
Perez Arribas, Goodwin, Geddes, Lyons, and
Saunders [42]
), Mathematical Finance (
Abi Jaber and Gérard [3]
,
Bayer, Hager, Riedel, and
Schoenmakers [7]
,
Bayer, Pelizzari, and Schoenmakers [8]
,
Cartea, Perez Arribas, and
Sanchez-Betancourt [11]
,
Cuchiero, Gazzani, Möller, and
Svaluto-Ferro [16]
,
Dupire and Tissot-Daguette [20]
,
Lyons, Nejad, and Arribas [38]
)…

Our motivation for studying exact representation formulae in the form (
1.1
) for path-dependent processes arises from the importance of non-Markovian stochastic modeling in various fields. Specifically, stochastic Volterra and delay equations capture intricate temporal dependencies in time series data. For such non-Markovian processes, the representation formulae (
1.1
) not only reveal the underlying Markovian structure in the whole signature process
\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}
but also enable us to re-frame them within a rather universal framework of linear functionals of the signature, for which one can develop generic methods. As illustration, recent works by
Abi Jaber and Gérard [3]
,
Cuchiero, Svaluto-Ferro, and
Teichmann [17]
have demonstrated interest in infinite linear combinations of signature elements, unveiling a surprising infinite-dimensional affine structure underlying a broad spectrum of stochastic models. Certain Fourier-Laplace transforms of linear functionals of the signature can be computed in terms of non-standard tensor valued infinite-dimensional Riccati equations and applied in practice for risk management purposes in mathematical finance for instance.

Related literature on series and functional expansions.
Stochastic Taylor expansions of solutions to Markovian stochastic differential equations (SDEs) in terms of the signature of the time augmented driving Brownian motion
(t,W_{t})_{t\geq 0}
have appeared in
Kloeden et al. [33, Chapter 5]
and have been used to develop a cubature formula on the Wiener space by
Lyons [39]
.
Ben Arous [9]
establishes convergence criterion for such stochastic Taylor series for Markovian SDEs with analytic coefficients; the so-called Chen–Fliess
[
23
,
24
]
approximation that generalizes the classic stochastic Taylor expansion for path dependent functionals of Markovian SDEs is studied by
Litterer and Oberhauser [35]
. A functional Taylor expansion in terms of a finite series of higher order functional derivatives multiplied by iterated integrals is presented by
Dupire and Tissot-Daguette [20]
, a nice overview on series and functional expansions is given there. Variation of constants formulae for linear forward and backward stochastic Volterra integral equations using certain sequence of iterated Volterra integrals, adapting Wiener–Itô chaos expansions, are derived by
Hamaguchi [29]
. Related finite order expansions also appeared in
Bayer, Friz, Gassiat, Martin, and
Stemper [6]
,
Hu [32]
and
Neuenkirch, Nourdin, Rößler, and
Tindel [40]
.

Our expansions can be seen as an infinite Stochastic-Taylor series expansion of the solution of path-dependent stochastic equations whose coefficients depend linearly on the whole trajectory. In particular, we believe that the coefficients of our time-independent expansions, obtained in ‘one shot’ by solving an algebraic equation, can be recovered from successive functional derivatives of the coefficients of the stochastic equation. None of the works cited above rely on such algebraic method. When it comes to studying the convergence of the stochastic Taylor series, our work is closest in spirit to
Ben Arous [9]
which operates in a Markovian setting. Just like Taylor expansions
[
33
,
20
]
, and unlike Volterra and Wiener expansions, the coefficients
\bm{\ell}
that depend on the model parameters and the signature
\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}
of the driving noise are disentangled. Combined with the explicit knowledge of the coefficients, this allows for a straightforward and simple numerical implementation of non-Markovian processes.

Moreover, finite expansions of Volterra and delay processes have also appeared in the rough path literature, using tailor-made constructions of signature objects.
We mention, for instance, Volterra-type signatures, where the iterated integrals involve weights coming from certain kernels, as developed in
Deya and Tindel [18]
,
Deya and Tindel [19]
,
Harang and Tindel [31]
,
Harang et al. [30]
,
Bruned and Katsetsiadis [10]
, as well as the discrete delay signature introduced in
Neuenkirch, Nourdin, and
Tindel [41]
. Compared to these works, in the present paper we do not rely on such specialized signature constructions.
Instead, we leverage the standard (time-augmented) signature, which offers several advantages:
it provides a unified and versatile framework for expanding both Volterra and delay processes,
it preserves the classical Chen identity and shuffle product,
and it yields a Markovian lift of our inherently non-Markovian dynamics, making it particularly convenient for computations (e.g. conditional moments in Section
5
). Beyond these considerations, we stress that our contribution goes significantly further than previous works that focus primarily on formal expansions. While existing literature, including Volterra-based approaches, typically produces finite stochastic Taylor expansions, we rigorously establish the convergence of the infinite signature expansion for the class of processes we consider.

Outline.
Section
2
deals with some preliminaries on path-signatures. Section
3
is concerned with infinite linear combinations of signature elements and their properties. Section
4
collects our representations formulae together with a numerical illustration. Section
5
presents series expansions for the moments. The proofs are postponed to later sections and to the Appendices.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we setup the framework for dealing with signatures of semimartingales. We not only collect some (well-known) properties of finite linear combinations of signature elements, refer also to the first sections in
[
7
,
17
,
38
]
, but we also introduce and study the resolvent object in Section
2.2
which plays a crucial role in our main results.

2.1
Tensor algebra

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
and denote by
\otimes
the tensor product over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, e.g.
(x\otimes y\otimes z)_{ijk}=x_{i}y_{j}z_{k}
, for
i,j,k=1,\dots,d
, for
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For
n\geq 1
, we denote by
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes n}
the space of tensors of order
n
and by
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes 0}=\mathbb{R}
. In the sequel, we will consider mathematical objects, path signatures, that live on the extended tensor algebra space
T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, that is the space of (infinite) sequences of tensors defined by

T((\mathbb{R}^{d})):=\left\{\bm{\ell}=(\bm{\ell}^{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}:\bm{\ell}^{n}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes n}\right\}.

Similarly, for
M\geq 0
, we define the truncated tensor algebra
T^{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as the space of sequences of tensors of order at most
M
defined by

T^{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\left\{\bm{\ell}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d})):\bm{\ell}^{n}=0,\quad\forall n>M\right\},

and the tensor algebra
T(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as the space of all finite sequences of tensors defined by

T(\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\bigcup_{M\in\mathbb{N}}T^{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

We clearly have
T(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. For
\bm{\ell}=(\bm{\ell}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},\bm{\varphi}=(\bm{\varphi}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the following operations:

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}+\bm{\varphi}:

\displaystyle=(\bm{\ell}^{n}+\bm{\varphi}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},\quad\bm{\ell}\otimes\bm{\varphi}:=\left(\sum_{k=0}^{n}\bm{\ell}^{k}\otimes\bm{\varphi}^{n-k}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}},\quad\lambda\bm{\ell}:=(\lambda\bm{\ell}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}.

For the rest of the paper, we will use
\bm{\ell}\bm{\varphi}
and
\bm{\ell}\otimes\bm{\varphi}
interchangeably.
These operations are closed on
T^{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
T(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Important notations.
Let
\left\{e_{1},\dots,e_{d}\right\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
A_{d}=\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},\dots,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{d}}}\right\}
be the corresponding alphabet. To ease reading, for
i\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
, we write
e_{i}
as the blue letter
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}
and for
n\geq 1,

i_{1},\dots,i_{n}\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
, we write
e_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{i_{n}}
as the concatenation of letters
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}}}
, that we call a word of length
n
. We note that the family
(e_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{i_{n}})_{(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}^{n}}
is a basis of
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes n}
that can be identified with the set of words of length
n
defined by

V_{n}:=\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i_{k}}}}\in A_{d}\text{ for }k=1,2,\dots,n\right\}.

(2.1)

Moreover, we denote by
ø
the empty word and we set
V_{0}=\left\{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}\right\}
which serves as a basis for
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes 0}=\mathbb{R}
. Equipped with the product topology,
T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
admits
V:=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}V_{n}
as a Schauder topological basis. In particular, every
\bm{\ell}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
can be decomposed as

\bm{\ell}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\ell}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},

(2.2)

where
\bm{\ell}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}}\in\mathbb{R}
is the real coefficient of
\bm{\ell}
at coordinate
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}
. Representation (
2.2
) induces a natural linear homeomorphism from
T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
to
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
, showing that it is a locally convex space. This representation will be frequently used in the paper. We stress again that in the sequel, every blue ‘word’
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V
represents an element of the canonical basis of
T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, i.e. there exists
n\geq 0
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}
is of the form
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}}}
, which represents the element
e_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{i_{n}}
. The concatenation
\bm{\ell}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}
of an element
\bm{\ell}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and the word
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}}}
means
\bm{\ell}\otimes e_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{i_{n}}
.

In addition to the decomposition (
2.2
) of elements
\bm{\ell}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, we introduce the projection
\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
as

\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}}:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\ell}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{vu}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}

(2.3)

for all
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\in V
. The projection plays an important role in the space of iterated integrals as it is closely linked to partial differentiation, in contrast with the concatenation that relates to integration. It will be used throughout the paper.

Remark 2.1
.

The projection allows us to decompose elements of the extended tensor algebra
\bm{\ell}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
as

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}=\bm{\ell}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}+\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}}\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}.

(2.4)

For instance, for
\bm{\ell}=4\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}+3\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}-1\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{12}}}+2\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2212}}}
, we have that
\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}}=4
,
\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}=3\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}
,
\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}}=-1\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+2\cdot{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{221}}}

and
\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{3}}}}=0
.

\blacksquare

We define the bracket between
\bm{\ell}\in T(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
{\mathbb{X}}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
by

\displaystyle\langle\bm{\ell},{\mathbb{X}}\rangle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}{\mathbb{X}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}.

(2.5)

Notice that it is well-defined as
\bm{\ell}
has finitely many non-zero terms. For
\bm{\ell}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, the series in (
2.5
) involves infinitely many terms and requires special care, this will be discussed in Section
3
below.

We will also consider another operation on the space of words
V
, the shuffle product. The shuffle product plays a crucial role for an integration by parts formula on the space of iterated integrals, see Lemma
2.5
below.

Definition 2.1

(Shuffle product)
.

The shuffle product
\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}:V\times V\to T(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined inductively for all words
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}
and all letters
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}
in
A_{d}
by

\displaystyle({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}})\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}})

\displaystyle=({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}})){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}+(({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}})\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}.

With some abuse of notation, the shuffle product on
T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
induced by the shuffle product on
V
will also be denoted by
\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}
. The shuffle product is clearly commutative. See
[
26
,
44
]
for more information on the shuffle product.

The shuffle product corresponds to the shuffling of two decks of cards together, while keeping the order of each single deck as illustrated on the following example:
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{23}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{123}}}+{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{213}}}+{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{231}}}
.

2.2
Resolvent and linear equation

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\bm{\ell}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, we define the concatenation power of
\bm{\ell}
by

\bm{\ell}^{\otimes n}:=\overbrace{\bm{\ell}\otimes\bm{\ell}\otimes\cdots\otimes\bm{\ell}}^{\text{$n$ times}},

with the convention
\bm{\ell}^{\otimes 0}={\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}
.

For every
\bm{\ell}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that
\bm{\ell}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}=0
, we define its
resolvent
by

{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{\ell}\right)}^{-1}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\bm{\ell}^{\otimes n}.

(2.6)

Remark 2.2
.

The condition
\bm{\ell}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}=0
can easily be loosened to
\bm{\ell}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}\neq 1
by scaling and shifting
\bm{\ell}
, i.e.

{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{\ell}\right)}^{-1}=\frac{1}{1-\bm{\ell}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}}{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\frac{1}{1-\bm{\ell}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}}\left(\bm{\ell}-\bm{\ell}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}\right)\right)}^{-1}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\left(1-\bm{\ell}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}\right)^{n+1}}\left(\bm{\ell}-\bm{\ell}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}\right)^{\otimes n}.

(2.7)

We thus write
{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{\ell}\right)}^{-1}
for all
\bm{\ell}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that
\bm{\ell}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}\neq 1
.

\blacksquare

Remark 2.3
.

It is clear that
\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{\ell}\right){\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{\ell}\right)}^{-1}={\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{\ell}\right)}^{-1}\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{\ell}\right)={\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}
.

\blacksquare

The purpose of the resolvent is to solve linear algebraic equations as shown in the next proposition.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
\bm{p},\bm{q}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that
\bm{q}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}\neq 1
, then the unique solution
\bm{\ell}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
to the linear algebraic equation

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}=\bm{p}+\bm{\ell}\bm{q}

(2.8)

is given by

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}=\bm{p}{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{q}\right)}^{-1}.

(2.9)

Proof.

It is easy to verify that
\bm{\ell}=\bm{p}{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{q}\right)}^{-1}
is a solution of (
2.8
). On the other hand, for every
\bm{\varphi}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
s.t.
\bm{\varphi}=\bm{p}+\bm{\varphi}\bm{q}
, we directly derive
\bm{\varphi}({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{q})=\bm{p}=\bm{\ell}({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{q})
and then
(\bm{\varphi}-\bm{\ell})({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{q})=0
. Hence
\bm{\varphi}=\bm{\ell}
, verifying the uniqueness.
∎

Interestingly, whenever
\bm{\ell}
is a linear combination of single letters, the resolvent of
\bm{\ell}
is equal to the shuffle exponential
e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{\ell}}
defined by

e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{\ell}}:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\bm{\ell}^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}n}}{n!}

(2.10)

with

\bm{\ell}^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}n}:=\overbrace{\bm{\ell}\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{\ell}\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\cdots\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{\ell}}^{\text{$n$ times}},\quad n\geq 1,\quad\bm{\ell}^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}0}={\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
\bm{b}=\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}}\bm{b}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}
, with
\bm{b}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in\mathbb{R}
, we have that

{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{b}\right)}^{-1}=e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{b}}.

(2.11)

In particular, this implies

e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{b}}={\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}+e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{b}}\bm{b}={\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}+\bm{b}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{b}}.

(2.12)

Proof.

We first observe that
\bm{b}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}}=0
, so
{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{b}\right)}^{-1}
is well-defined. We then prove that
\frac{1}{k!}\bm{b}^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}k}=\bm{b}^{\otimes k}
.
By induction, it suffices to prove for every
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,

\bm{b}^{\otimes k-1}\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{b}=k\bm{b}^{\otimes k}.

(2.13)

It is easy to check that (
2.13
) is satisfied for
k=1
. Now, assuming it holds for some
k\geq 1
, we can deduce

\displaystyle\bm{b}^{\otimes k}\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{b}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}}\bm{b}^{\otimes k}\bm{b}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}+\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}}\left(\bm{b}^{\otimes k}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{b}\right){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}

(2.14)

\displaystyle=\bm{b}^{\otimes k+1}+\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}}\left(\bm{b}^{\otimes k-1}\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{b}\right)\bm{b}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}

(2.15)

\displaystyle=\bm{b}^{\otimes k+1}+\left(\bm{b}^{\otimes k-1}\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{b}\right)\bm{b}

(2.16)

\displaystyle=\bm{b}^{\otimes k+1}+(k\bm{b}^{\otimes k})\bm{b}

(2.17)

\displaystyle=(k+1)\bm{b}^{\otimes k+1},

(2.18)

which proves
(
2.13
) by induction, hence proving the equality (
2.11
). Noticing that
{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{b}\right)}^{-1}={\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}+\bm{b}{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{b}\right)}^{-1}={\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}+{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{b}\right)}^{-1}\bm{b}
, we derive (
2.12
).
∎

2.3
Signature of a continuous semimartingale

We define the (path) signature of a semimartingale as the sequence of iterated stochastic integrals in the sense of Stratonovich. Throughout the paper, the Itô integral is denoted by
\int_{0}^{\cdot}Y_{t}\mathrm{d}X_{t}
and the Stratonovich integral by
\int_{0}^{\cdot}Y_{t}\circ\mathrm{d}X_{t}
. If both
X
and
Y
are semimartingales then,
\int_{0}^{\cdot}Y_{t}\circ\mathrm{d}X_{t}:=\int_{0}^{\cdot}Y_{t}\mathrm{d}X_{t}+\frac{1}{2}[X,Y]_{\cdot}
.

Definition 2.4

(Signature)
.

Fix
T>0
.
Let
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a continuous semimartingale on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
on a complete filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0},\mathbb{P})
. The signature of
X
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathbb{X}:\Omega\times[0,T]

\displaystyle\to T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))

\displaystyle(\omega,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbb{X}_{t}(\omega):=(1,\mathbb{X}_{t}^{1}(\omega),\dots,\mathbb{X}_{t}^{n}(\omega),\dots),

where

\mathbb{X}_{t}^{n}:=\int_{0<u_{1}<\cdots<u_{n}<t}\circ\mathrm{d}X_{u_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\circ\mathrm{d}X_{u_{n}}

takes value in
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes n}
,
n\geq 0
. Similarly, the truncated signature of order
M\in\mathbb{N}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathbb{X}^{\leq M}:[0,T]

\displaystyle\to T^{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

(2.19)

\displaystyle(\omega,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbb{X}_{t}^{\leq M}(\omega):=(1,\mathbb{X}_{t}^{1}(\omega),\dots,\mathbb{X}_{t}^{M}(\omega),0,\dots,0,\dots).

(2.20)

The signature plays a similar role as polynomials on path-space. Indeed, in dimension
d=1
, the signature of
X
is the sequence of monomials
\left(\frac{(X_{t}-X_{0})^{n}}{n!}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. In particular, for
\bm{\ell}\in T^{M}(\mathbb{R})
,
\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle
is a polynomial in
(X_{t}-X_{0})
of degree no greater than
M
.

Example 2.4
.

For
X_{t}=(t,W_{t}^{1},W_{t}^{2},\dots,W_{t}^{d})
, where
(W_{t}^{1},W_{t}^{2},\dots,W_{t}^{d})
is a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion, we can compute the first level of the signature

\mathbb{X}_{t}^{1}=\left(t,W_{t}^{1},W_{t}^{2},\cdots,W_{t}^{d}\right),

and the second level

\mathbb{X}_{t}^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{t^{2}}{2}&\int_{0}^{t}s\mathrm{d}W^{1}_{s}&\cdots&\int_{0}^{t}s\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{d}\\
\int_{0}^{t}W_{s}^{1}\mathrm{d}s&\frac{\left(W_{t}^{1}\right)^{2}}{2}&\cdots&\int_{0}^{t}W_{s}^{1}\circ\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{d}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\int_{0}^{t}W_{s}^{d}\mathrm{d}s&\int_{0}^{t}W_{s}^{d}\circ\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{1}&\cdots&\frac{\left(W_{t}^{d}\right)^{2}}{2}\end{pmatrix}.

\blacksquare

Remark 2.5
.

It follows from Definition
2.4
that the level
n
of the signature
\mathbb{X}_{t}^{n}:=(\mathbb{X}_{t}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}}}})_{({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}}})\in V_{n}}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes n}
can be written in the following iterated form

\displaystyle\mathbb{X}_{t}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}}}}=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{X}_{s}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i_{1}\cdots i_{n-1}}}}}\circ\mathrm{d}X_{s}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i_{n}}}}},\quad{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i_{1}\cdots i_{n}}}}}\in V_{n}.

(2.21)

\blacksquare

2.4
Finite linear combinations of signature elements

In this section, we fix
X
a continuous semimartingale on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and we let
\mathbb{X}
be its signature. We recall some important properties of finite linear combinations of signature elements
\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle
for
\bm{\ell}\in T(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, recall (
2.5
).

The first one highlights a crucial linearization property of the signature, the product of two linear combinations of the signature is again a linear combination of the signature where the coefficients are given by the shuffle product, recall Definition
2.1
. This property will be later extended to infinite linear combinations of signatures in Proposition
3.1
below.

Lemma 2.5

(Shuffle product property)
.

For
\bm{\ell},\bm{\varphi}\in T(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle\left\langle\bm{\varphi},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle=\left\langle\bm{\ell}\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{\varphi},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle.

Proof.

This follows from the standard chain rule of Stratonovich integrals, see
Gaines [26, Proposition 2.2]
.
∎

The shuffle product can be seen as an extension of the Cauchy product to the space of signatures. For example, set
d=1
,
X_{0}=0
and
\bm{\ell}\in T^{M}(\mathbb{R}),\bm{\varphi}\in T^{N}(\mathbb{R})
for some
N,M\in\mathbb{N}
, i.e. there exists
(a_{m})_{m\leq M},(b_{n})_{n\leq N}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\bm{\ell}=\sum_{m=0}^{M}a_{m}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}^{\otimes m}
,
\bm{\varphi}=\sum_{n=0}^{N}b_{n}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}^{\otimes n}
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle\left\langle\bm{\varphi},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{M}a_{m}\frac{(X_{t})^{m}}{m!}\sum_{n=0}^{N}b_{n}\frac{(X_{t})^{n}}{n!}=\sum_{k=0}^{M+N}c_{k}\frac{(X_{t})^{k}}{k!}

with
c_{k}=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\binom{k}{i}a_{i}b_{k-i}
. In this case
\bm{\ell}\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{\varphi}=\sum_{k=0}^{M+N}c_{k}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}^{\otimes k}
.

The second property shows that any finite linear combination of signature elements is a semi-martingale with an explicit decomposition given in terms of the projection elements, recall (
2.3
). This semi-martingality property is no longer always true when infinite linear combinations of signature elements are considered, see Theorem
3.3
below.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\bm{\ell}\in T(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
(\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle)_{t\geq 0}
is a semimartingale with decomposition

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle=\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}}\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle\circ\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}.

(2.22)

Proof.

It follows from the equivalent definition of the signature (
2.21
) that

\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle=\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\circ\mathrm{d}X_{s}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}},\quad{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}.

Then, using the projection relation (
2.3
) and the linearity of the bracket, we get that

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle=\left\langle\bm{\ell}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}+\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}}\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle=\bm{\ell}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}+\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\circ\mathrm{d}X_{s}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}.

∎

Finally, we show how to transform the Stratonovich integral into the Itô integral in the context of finite linear combination of the signature.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\bm{\ell}\in T(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then for every
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}
:

\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle\circ\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}=\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in A_{d}}\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}[X^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}},X^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}]_{t}.

Proof.

Denote by
Y_{t}=\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle
and fix
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}
. It follows from Lemma
2.6
that
Y
is a semimartingale with dynamics

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}Y_{t}=\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in A_{d}}\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle\circ\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}.

Therefore, by the definition of the Stratonovich integral:

\displaystyle Y_{t}\circ\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}=Y_{t}\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{d}[Y,X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}]_{t}=Y_{t}\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in A_{d}}\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}[X^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}},X^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}]_{t}.

∎

3
Infinite linear combinations of signature elements

From now on, we fix a complete filtered probability space (
\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F}:=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]},\mathbb{P}
) satisfying the usual conditions, and
\left(X_{t}\right)_{0\leq t\leq T}
a continuous
\mathbb{F}
-semimartingale on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and we let
\mathbb{X}
be its signature. In Section
2.3
, we recalled well-known properties of finite linear combinations of signature elements
\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle
for
\bm{\ell}\in T(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In this section, we study infinite linear combinations
\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle
for certain admissible
\bm{\ell}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for which the infinite series will make sense. Our central results in this section are an Itô formula for such elements in Theorem
3.3
below, a control for their supremum norm in Propositions
3.5
and
3.6
, and an estimate of solutions of algebraic linear equations taking values in the extended tensor algebra in Theorem
3.9
.

For this, we consider the space
\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{X})
of admissible elements
\bm{\ell}
using the associated stochastic semi-norm:

\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{X})}:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left|\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\mathbb{X}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\right|,\quad t\geq 0,

recall the definition of
V_{n}
in (
2.1
) and the decomposition (
2.2
).
\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{X})}
is actually random variables. Whenever
\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{X})}<\infty
a.s., the infinite linear combination

\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\mathbb{X}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}

is well-defined. This leads to the following definition for the admissible set
\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{X}):

\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{X}):=\left\{\bm{\ell}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d})):\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{X})}<\infty\text{ for all }t\in[0,T]\text{ a.s.}\right\}.

For simplicity, we write
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}
), in place of
\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{X})
(resp.
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{X})}
), when it does not cause ambiguity. Note that
T(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset\mathcal{A}
and that
\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle
extends (
2.5
), as the two bracket operations
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
coincide whenever
\bm{\ell}\in T(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Remark 3.1
.

The space
\mathcal{A}
plays a crucial role in this paper. Whenever we talk about
\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle
for
\bm{\ell}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, we have to first verify that
\bm{\ell}
belongs to
\mathcal{A}
to ensure that
\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle
is well-defined. The set
\mathcal{A}
is a natural set to consider. It has also been studied in
Cuchiero, Svaluto-Ferro, and
Teichmann [17, Example 3.2 (iii)]
. In practice, however, we will use a stronger but more tractable criterion in Section
3.2
below to obtain that a given
\bm{\ell}
belongs to
\mathcal{A}
.

\blacksquare

Remark 3.2
.

If
\bm{\ell}\in\mathcal{A}
, then
\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle
is a progressively measurable process, as it is the a.s.-limit of a series of progressively measurable processes.

\blacksquare

Remark 3.3
.

We could restrict to a slightly smaller space
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
:

\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbb{X}):=\left\{\bm{\ell}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d})):\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{X})}<\infty\text{ a.s.}\right\},

and show that whenever
X
and
Y
follow the same law,
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbb{X})=\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbb{Y})
. This would however not be the case in general for
\mathcal{A}
due to measurability issue.

\blacksquare

It is straightforward to see that the sets
\mathcal{A}
is closed under linear operations, i.e. for
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
and
\bm{\ell},\bm{\varphi}\in\mathcal{A}
, then
\alpha\bm{\ell}+\beta\bm{\varphi}\in\mathcal{A}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{A}
is closed under the shuffle product.

The next proposition extends the shuffle property, derived in Proposition
2.5
, to the case of infinite linear combination of signatures. We note that it can be obtained from a particular instance of the more general result in
[
17
, Lemma 4.4]
dealing with so-called shuffle compatible partitions. For completeness we provide a proof for our specific setting in Appendix
C
.

Proposition 3.1

(Extended Shuffle property)
.

If
\bm{\ell},\bm{\varphi}\in\mathcal{A}
, then
\bm{\ell}\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{\varphi}\in\mathcal{A}
and

\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle\left\langle\bm{\varphi},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle=\left\langle\bm{\ell}\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{\varphi},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle.

Proof.

The proof is provided in Appendix
C
.
∎

3.1
The space
\mathcal{I}
and Itô’s formula

The aim of this subsection is the derivation of an Itô’s formula in Theorem
3.3
below for infinite linear combinations of signature elements. In preparation, we define
\mathcal{Q}_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}(X)
, the set of coordinates of
X
that have a non-zero quadratic covariation with
X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}
:

\mathcal{Q}_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}(X):=\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in A_{d}:[X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}},X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}]_{t}\neq 0,\,\text{on a set of non-zero $\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}$ measure}\right\},\quad{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}.

Remark 3.4
.

\mathcal{Q}_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}(X)
is introduced in order to get rid of unnecessary assumptions in Lemma
3.2
, since we only need
\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}}\in\mathcal{A}
for those
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in\mathcal{Q}_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}(X)
. For example, in the particular case of
X_{t}=(t,W_{t})
, with
W
a one-dimensional Brownian motion, assumptions are only needed for the terms
\bm{\ell},\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}},\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}},\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{22}}}}\in\mathcal{A}
, but not for
\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{21}}}}
and
\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{12}}}}
.

\blacksquare

Lemma 3.2
.

Fix
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}
and let
\bm{\ell}\in\mathcal{A}
be such that

\bm{\ell}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in\mathcal{A},\quad\int_{0}^{T}\left(\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}[X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}},X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}]_{t}<\infty\text{ and }\int_{0}^{T}\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{d}\left|F_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\right|<\infty,

(3.1)

where
F^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}
is the finite variation part of
X^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}
. We also assume that for every
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in\mathcal{Q}_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}(X)
,

\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}}\in\mathcal{A}\quad\text{and}\quad\int^{T}_{0}\left|\left|\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{d}[X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}},X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}]_{t}<\infty.

(3.2)

Then,

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{\ell}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle=\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}X_{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in A_{d}}\int^{t}_{0}\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}[X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}},X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}]_{s},\quad\text{a.s.}

(3.3)

for every
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof.

Fix
t\in[0,T]
. Clearly, under the assumptions (
3.1
)-(
3.2
) on
\bm{\ell}
, the right side of equation (
3.3
) is well-defined. For every word
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V
, we can write it as
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}=\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in A_{d}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}
. By Lemma
2.7
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{vi}}},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle=\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle\circ\mathrm{d}X^{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}_{t}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in A_{d}}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}[X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}},X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}]_{s}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}X_{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}.

(3.4)

Combined with the decomposition (
2.2
), it yields that

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{\ell}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{vi}}},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in A_{d}}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}[X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}},X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}]_{s}+\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}X_{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\right).

(3.5)

It remains to argue, using dominated convergence, that both terms appearing on the right-hand side of (
3.5
) correspond to the terms in (
3.3
).

(
i
)

For the first term, if
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\notin\mathcal{Q}_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}(X)
,
[X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}},X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}]_{t}=0
for all
t
, so we only care about the letters
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in\mathcal{Q}_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}(X)
. Since
\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}}\in\mathcal{A}
by assumption (
3.2
), we can apply the dominated convergence theorem, with
\left|\left|\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}
as the dominated function, and get

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}[X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}},X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}]_{s}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\right)\mathrm{d}[X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}},X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}]_{s}

(3.6)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}[X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}},X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}]_{s}

(3.7)

(
ii
)

For the second term, let us define

Y_{t}^{N}:=\sum_{n=0}^{N}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}X_{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}.

On the one hand, as
\int_{0}^{T}\left(\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}[X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}},X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}]_{t}<\infty
and
\sum_{n=0}^{N}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle\leq\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}
for every
N
and assumption (
3.1
), by the dominated convergence theorem for stochastic integrals, the random variable
Y^{N}
converges in probability to

Y_{t}^{\infty}:=\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}X_{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}

as
N\to\infty
.

On the other hand, since
\bm{\ell}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in\mathcal{A}
, the left-hand side of (
3.5
) is a.s. finite, and since second term of its right-hand side has been proved to be finite, it follows that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}X_{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}
exists a.s. and corresponds to an almost sure limit of
Y^{N}
as
N\to\infty
. Which yields

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}X_{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}=Y^{\infty}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}X_{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\quad{a.s.}

(3.8)

Combining (
3.5
), (
3.7
) and (
3.8
) yields (
3.3
) and completes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

To obtain that equality (
3.3
) holds for every
t
almost surely, one could argue that
\left\langle\bm{\ell}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle
is a continuous process for instance. This requires additional conditions on
\bm{\ell}
, see for instance Proposition
3.6
-
(iii)
below.

\blacksquare

We are now in place to state the main result of this section which is an Itô’s formula for infinite linear combinations of signature elements. For this, we need to define the following set

\mathcal{I}(X):=\left\{\bm{\ell}\in\mathcal{A}:\begin{matrix}\text{for every }{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}\text{ and }{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in\mathcal{Q}_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}(X),\;\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}},\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{ji}}}}\in\mathcal{A}\text{ and }\hfill\\
\int_{0}^{T}\left(\left|\left|\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{d}\left|F_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\right|+\left|\left|\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{ji}}}}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{d}[X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}},X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}]_{t}+\left(\left|\left|\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}[X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}},X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}]_{t}\right)<\infty,\;\text{a.s.}\hfill\end{matrix}\right\},

(3.9)

which can be seen as the analog of the space
C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
when one applies Itô’s formula on
f(W)
, see Example
3.6
below.

Theorem 3.3

(Itô’s formula)
.

Let
\bm{\ell}\in\mathcal{I}(X)
, then
(\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle)_{t\leq T}
is an Itô process such that

\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle=\bm{\ell}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}+\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}X_{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in A_{d}}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{ji}}}},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}[X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}},X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}]_{s},

(3.10)

for all
t\leq T
. In particular, when we take
X_{t}
to be
\widehat{W}_{t}=(t,W^{2}_{t},\ldots,W^{d}_{t})
, with
W
a
(d-1)
-Brownian motion then, the set
\mathcal{I}
reads

\mathcal{I}(\widehat{W})=\left\{\bm{\ell}\in\mathcal{A}:\begin{matrix}\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}},\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}},\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{jj}}}}\in\mathcal{A},\;{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},\dots,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{d}}}\right\},\text{ and }\hfill\\
\int_{0}^{T}\left|\left|\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},\dots,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{d}}}\right\}}\int_{0}^{T}\left(\left|\left|\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{jj}}}}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}+\left(\left|\left|\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}\right)^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}t<\infty,\;\text{a.s.}\hfill\end{matrix}\right\},

(3.11)

and for every
\bm{\ell}\in\mathcal{I}(\widehat{W})
:

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{\ell},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle=\bm{\ell}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},\dots,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{d}}}\right\}}\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{jj}}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s+\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},\dots,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{d}}}\right\}}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}.

(3.12)

Proof.

The proof follows from an application of Lemma
3.2
combined with the decomposition (
2.3
).
∎

The following example highlights that Theorem
3.3
is indeed an extension of the usual Itô’s formula on
f(W)
.

Example 3.6
.

Let
d=2
and
\widehat{W}_{t}=(t,W_{t})
, and fix an analytic function
f(y):=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n}y^{n}
with infinite radius of convergence which we apply to
W
. It is clear that

f(W_{t})=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n}W_{t}^{n}=\left\langle\bm{\ell},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle,

where

\bm{\ell}=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}n}=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n}n!{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}^{\otimes n}.

In particular, the projections read:

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n+1}(n+1)!{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}^{\otimes n}=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n+1}(n+1){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}n},

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{22}}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n+2}(n+2)!{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}^{\otimes n}=\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n+2}(n+1)(n+2){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}n}.

It is easy to verify that
\bm{\ell},\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}},\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{22}}}}\in\mathcal{A}
since
f
has infinite radius of convergence. We can thus further derive that

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(W_{t})=\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle,\quad f^{\prime\prime}(W_{t})=\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{22}}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle.

On the other hand we can see that
\left|\left|\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+1)|a_{n+1}|\cdot|W_{t}|^{n}
, and since
g(x):=\sum_{n\geq 0}(n+1)|a_{n+1}|x^{n}
is also analytic and
\left|\left|\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}
has continuous sample path almost surely, then

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\left|\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}<\infty.

With similar arguments we can show that
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\left|\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{22}}}}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}<\infty.
This allows us to verify that
\bm{\ell}\in\mathcal{I}(\widehat{W})
. An application of our Itô’s formula (
3.12
) yields

\left\langle\bm{\ell},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle=\bm{\ell}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}+\int^{t}_{0}\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}W_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{22}}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s,

which translates into

f(W_{t})=f(0)+\int_{0}^{t}f^{\prime}(W_{s})\mathrm{d}W_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}f^{\prime\prime}(W_{s})\mathrm{d}s,

the standard Itô’s formula.

\blacksquare

Remark 3.7
.

It should be noted that the identity (
3.10
) only holds almost surely for every fixed
t
. However, as remark
3.5
, if we additionally assume
\left\langle\bm{\ell},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle
is continuous, then (
3.10
) holds for every
t\in[0,T]
almost surely. In particular, when
X_{t}=\widehat{W}_{t}=(t,W_{t}^{2},\dots,W_{t}^{d})
, and
\bm{\ell}\in{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
(where
{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
is introduced in Section
3.2
below), the continuity assumption is satisfied by Proposition (
3.6
)-
(iii)
. Our applications of Theorem
3.3
in later section are all in this strong sense.

\blacksquare

We can easily extend Theorem
3.3
to time-dependent linear functionals, i.e.
\bm{\ell}:[0,T]\to T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. For this, let us define

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}(X):=\left\{\bm{\ell}:[0,T]\to\mathcal{A}:\begin{matrix}\text{for every }{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V,\>\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in C^{1}([0,T],\mathbb{R})\text{ and }\hfill\\
\text{for every }{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d},\,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in\mathcal{Q}_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}(X)\text{ and for all }t\in[0,T],\;\bm{\ell}_{t}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}},\bm{\ell}_{t}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{ji}}}},\dot{\bm{\ell}_{t}}\in\mathcal{A}\text{ and }\hfill\\
\int_{0}^{T}\left(\left|\left|\bm{\ell}_{t}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{d}\left|F_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\right|+\left|\left|\bm{\ell}_{t}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{ji}}}}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{d}[X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}},X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}]_{t}+\left(\left|\left|\bm{\ell}_{t}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}[X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}},X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}]_{t}\right)<\infty\;\text{ a.s.}\end{matrix}\right\},

(3.13)

where
\dot{\bm{\ell}_{t}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\bm{\ell}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}
.

Corollary 3.4
.

Let
\bm{\ell}\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}(X)
, then
(\left\langle\bm{\ell}_{t},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle)_{t\leq T}
is an Itô process such that

\left\langle\bm{\ell}_{t},\mathbb{X}_{t}\right\rangle=\bm{\ell}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}}+\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\ell}_{s}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}X_{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in A_{d}}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}}\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\bm{\ell}_{s}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{ji}}}},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}[X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}},X^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}]_{s}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\dot{\bm{\ell}}_{s},\mathbb{X}_{s}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s,

(3.14)

for all
t\leq T.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Theorem
3.3
.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

The example of the fractional Riemann-Liouville Brownian motion, treated in Section
4.3
, shows that, contrary to the finite case, not all infinite linear combinations of signature elements of
\widehat{W}
are semimartingales. In particular, Theorem
3.3
and Corollary
3.4
cannot be applied in such cases.

\blacksquare

3.2
The space
{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
and moment estimates

In practice, it is not an easy task to verify directly whether a given
\bm{\ell}
belongs to
\mathcal{A}
, even for the case of the time extended
(d-1)
-dimensional Brownian motion
X_{t}=\widehat{W}_{t}=(t,W_{t}^{2},\dots,W_{t}^{d})
. The aim of this subsection is to introduce a subset
{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
of
\mathcal{A}
, specific to such
\widehat{W}
with
d\geq 2
, by essentially controlling the expectation of its signature
\widehat{\mathbb{W}}
. We will give a tractable criterion to prove that a given
\bm{\ell}
belongs to
{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
and thus to
\mathcal{A}
.

In order to motivate the set
{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
, we will derive an upper bound for the moments of the signature elements of
\widehat{\mathbb{W}}
with the help of Fawcett’s formula
[
21
]
using a deterministic function
h
. For that and to ease the notations, for every
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V
we let
n({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})
denote length of
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}
, i.e.
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})}
, and
x({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})
the number of occurrences of the letter
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}
in
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}
. In the following, if it does not cause ambiguity, we will write
n
and
x
in place of
n({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})
and
x({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})
respectively for brevity.

Example 3.9
.

Let
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{111321}}}
, then
n=6
and
x=4
.

\blacksquare

We first provide a tight bound for the second moment:

Proposition 3.5
.

Fix
t\in[0,T]
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V
. We have

(
i
)

\mathbb{E}\left|\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle\right|^{2}\leq\binom{2n}{n}\frac{t^{n+x}}{(n+x)!}2^{x-n},

(3.15)

(
ii
)

and there exists a non-negative constant
C\geq 2
independent of
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}
and
t
, such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\left|\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\right\rangle\right|^{2}\right]\leq h_{t}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})^{2},

(3.16)

where

h_{t}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}):=\frac{C}{({n+1})^{\frac{1}{4}}}{\frac{(2t)^{\frac{1}{2}(n+x)}}{\sqrt{(n+x-1)!}}},

with the convention that
n!=1
for
n\leq 0
. In particular,
h
satisfies the following sub-multiplicative property

\displaystyle h_{t}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})\leq h_{t}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}})h_{t}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}),\quad{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V.

(3.17)

Proof.

The proof is given in Appendix
B
.
∎

Based on Proposition
3.5
, an application of Jensen’s inequality yields

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\left|\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\right\rangle\right|\right]\leq h_{t}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}).

(3.18)

We are now ready to introduce a norm on
T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
using the function
h
:

\displaystyle\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\left|\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\right|h_{t}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}),\quad\bm{\ell}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d})).

(3.19)

Unlike
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|^{\mathcal{A}}
,
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
is deterministic, thus simplifying the computation. Moreover, notice that
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
is an increasing function of
t
, which naturally leads to the definition of the normed vector space:

{\mathcal{A}_{h}}:=\left\{\bm{\ell}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d})):\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{T}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}<\infty\right\}.

Remark 3.10
.

We can see that
{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
is a Banach space by simply constructing an isometry from
{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
to
\left\{(a_{i})_{i=0}^{\infty}:\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\left|a_{i}\right|<\infty\right\}
, and in particular it is a Köthe sequence space, see
[
34
,
43
]
.

\blacksquare

Proposition 3.6
.

Fix
\bm{\ell}\in{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
. The following properties hold:

(
i
)

{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\subset\mathcal{A}
, and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{s}^{\mathcal{A}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}},\quad t\leq T.

(3.20)

(
ii
)

{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
induces a natural upper bound

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\left\langle\bm{\ell},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\right\rangle^{2}\right]\leq\left(\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\right)^{2},\quad t\leq T.

(
iii
)

t\mapsto\left\langle\bm{\ell},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle
is continuous and the strong error of convergence of the truncated linear combination of the signature elements satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\left\langle\bm{\ell},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}^{\leq M}\right\rangle-\left\langle\bm{\ell},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle\right|\right]\leq\sum_{n=M}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\left|\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\right|h_{T}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}),\quad M\in\mathbb{N}.

(3.21)

Proof.

(
i
)

For all
\bm{\ell}\in{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
, the inequality (
3.18
) yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{s}^{\mathcal{A}}\right]\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\left|\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\right|\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\left|\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\right\rangle\right|\right]\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\left|\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\right|h_{t}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})\leq\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}<\infty.

(3.22)

Consequently,
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}}<\infty\text{ a.s.}
and thus
\bm{\ell}\in\mathcal{A}(\widehat{\mathbb{W}})
.

(
ii
)

Starting with

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{\ell},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\right\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\right\rangle\right)^{2}\leq\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\in V}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\in V}\left|\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\right|\cdot\left|\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\right\rangle\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\right\rangle\right|,

we get that

\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\left\langle\bm{\ell},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\right\rangle^{2}\leq\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\in V}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\in V}\left|\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\right|\cdot\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\left|\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\right\rangle\right|\cdot\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\left|\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\right\rangle\right|.

Taking the expectation and applying Cauchy-Schwarz inequality yields that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\left\langle\bm{\ell},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\right\rangle^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\in V}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\in V}\left|\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\right|\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\right\rangle^{2}}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\right\rangle^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V}\left|\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\right|\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\right\rangle^{2}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V}\left|\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\right|h_{t}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\right)^{2}.

(
iii
)

Define

\displaystyle X_{t}:=\left\langle\bm{\ell},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\quad\text{and}\quad X_{t}^{M}:=\left\langle\bm{\ell},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}^{\leq M}\right\rangle=\sum_{n=0}^{M}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}.

(3.23)

It follows from inequality (
3.18
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|X_{t}-X_{t}^{M}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=M+1}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\left|\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\right|\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle\right]\leq\sum_{n=M+1}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\left|\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\right|h_{T}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}).

(3.24)

Moreover,
\bm{\ell}\in{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
implies that

\lim_{M\to\infty}\sum_{n=M+1}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\left|\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\right|h_{T}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})=0.

Thus

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|X_{t}-X_{t}^{M}\right|\right]\to 0,

(3.25)

implying that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|X_{t}-X_{t}^{M}\right|\to 0\quad\text{in probability as $M\to\infty$.}

So there exists a sub-sequence
(M_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|X_{t}-X_{t}^{M_{k}}\right|\to 0
a.s. Moreover, as
X^{M_{k}}
is continuous,
X
is also continuous.

∎

Derived from (
3.17
), the next proposition shows that
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
is sub-multiplicative for the tensor product.

Proposition 3.7
.

For every
\bm{\ell},\bm{\varphi}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,

\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\bm{\varphi}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\leq\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\left|\left|\bm{\varphi}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}.

In particular, if
\bm{\ell},\bm{\varphi}\in{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
, then
\bm{\ell}\bm{\varphi}\in{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
.

Proof.

First notice that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\in V_{k}
,

\left\langle\bm{\ell}\bm{\varphi},{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\right\rangle=\sum_{n=0}^{k}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\in V_{n}}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{k-n}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\bm{\varphi}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\right\rangle,

so that

\displaystyle\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\bm{\varphi}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\in V_{k}}\left|\left\langle\bm{\ell}\bm{\varphi},{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\right\rangle\right|h_{t}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}})

(3.26)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\in V_{k}}\left|\sum_{n=0}^{k}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\in V_{n}}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{k-n}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\bm{\varphi}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\right\rangle\right|h_{t}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}})

(3.27)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{k}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\in V_{n}}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{k-n}}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\in V_{k}}\left|\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\bm{\varphi}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\right|\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\right\rangle h_{t}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}})

(3.28)

Then, using (
3.17
)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\bm{\varphi}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{k}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\in V_{n}}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{k-n}}\left|\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\bm{\varphi}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\right|h_{t}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}})h_{t}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\in V_{k}}\left\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\right\rangle

(3.29)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\in V_{n}}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{m}}\left|\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\bm{\varphi}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\right|h_{t}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}})h_{t}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})

(3.30)

\displaystyle=\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\left|\left|\bm{\varphi}\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}.

(3.31)

∎

Remark 3.11
.
