Compared to the literature, our estimate for the supremum norm (
3.16
) appears to be used for the first time and plays a crucial role in our convergence analysis. The closest related results appeared in
Ben Arous [9]
where moment estimates of
\mathbb{E}|\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\rangle|^{2}
and certain convergence criterion for infinite series of signatures of
\widehat{W}
, i.e. iterated sequence of Stratonovich integrals, are established. The estimate in
[
9
, Lemma 2]
has the same order as (
3.15
) given here, although the constant there is not as sharp as ours (since we make use of Fawcett’s formula) but has the same order of magnitude (see the remark following
[
9
, Lemma 3]
). We also provide estimates for
\mathbb{E}|\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\rangle|^{p}
for every
p\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, see proposition
B.1
. But most importantly, we provide an estimate for
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{s\in[0,t]}|\langle{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{s}\rangle|^{2}]
in (
3.16
), which is necessary to prove convergence for all
t
almost surely instead of simply for a fixed
t
, when compared to the convergence criterion in
[
9
, Theorem 2 (1)]
.
Additionally, the second criteria
[
9
, Theorem 2 (2)]
requires the second order norm to be bounded, we only require the first order norm
\left|\left|\cdot\right|\right|_{t}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
to be bounded, which is hence milder.

\blacksquare

3.3
Back to the linear algebraic equation

We now prove that the solution
\bm{\ell}
to the linear algebraic equation (
2.8
) belongs to
{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
under suitable conditions on
\bm{p}
and
\bm{q}
, see Theorem
3.9
. This is a crucial ingredient in obtaining convergence of our representations formula
\left\langle\bm{\ell},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle
in Section
4
. For this, we start by introducing the following partial order:

Definition 3.8
.

Let
\bm{\ell},\bm{\varphi}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. We say that
\bm{\ell}
is dominated by
\bm{\varphi}
if for all
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V
,
\left|\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\right|\leq\bm{\varphi}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}
. We then write

\bm{\ell}\preceq\bm{\varphi}.

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
\bm{p}\in{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
, let
\bm{q}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
be such that
\left|\bm{q}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}\right|<1
and assume there exists
\bm{\varphi}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that

\displaystyle\bm{q}\preceq\bm{\varphi}\quad\text{and}\quad{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{\varphi}\right)}^{-1}\in{\mathcal{A}_{h}}.

(3.32)

Then, the resolvent
{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{q}\right)}^{-1}
is well-defined and belongs to
{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
. Furthermore, the unique solution
\bm{\ell}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
to the linear equation

\bm{\ell}=\bm{p}+\bm{\ell}\bm{q}

belongs to
{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
.

In order to prove Theorem
3.9
, we will first show that the resolvent is order-preserving. That is:

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
\bm{q},\bm{\varphi}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that
\left|\bm{q}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}\right|,\bm{\varphi}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}<1
and
\bm{q}\preceq\bm{\varphi}
, then

{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{q}\right)}^{-1}\preceq{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{\varphi}\right)}^{-1}.

Proof.

We will first rewrite the equation of the resolvent (
2.6
) as

\displaystyle{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{q}\right)}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\bm{q}^{\otimes k}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{m}}\bm{q}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\right)^{\otimes k}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{m}}f_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}(\bm{q}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}},n({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}})\leq m){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}

(3.33)

where
f_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}
is a polynomial function of degree
n
for all
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}
, which is independent of
\bm{q}
and has
\frac{d^{n+1}-1}{d-1}
variables
\left\{\bm{q}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}:\left|{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\right|\leq\left|{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\right|\right\}
. We also notice that the coefficients of
f_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}}
are all non-negative integers. (For example,
f_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{11}}}(\bm{q}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}},|{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}|\leq 2)=2\bm{q}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}\bm{q}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{11}}}+(\bm{q}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}})^{2}
and
f_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{12}}}(\bm{q}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}},|{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}|\leq 2)=2\bm{q}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}\bm{q}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{12}}}+2\bm{q}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}\bm{q}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}
.)
Now recall the Definition
3.8
where
\bm{q}\preceq\bm{\varphi}
implies
\left|\bm{q}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\right|\leq\bm{\varphi}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}
for all
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V
. Combined this with the fact that
f
has non-negative coefficients, it is easy to see that, for all
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{m}
we have

\left|f_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}(\bm{q}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}},n({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}})\leq m)\right|\leq f_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}(\bm{\varphi}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}},n({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}})\leq m),

which ends the proof.
∎

We can now prove Theorem
3.9
.

Proof of Theorem
3.9
.

By assumption on
\bm{q}
and by Lemma
3.10
,
{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{q}\right)}^{-1}
is well-defined and belongs to
{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
. Moreover, by Proposition
2.2

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}=\bm{p}{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{q}\right)}^{-1}.

(3.34)

Finally, by assumption on
\bm{p}
and Proposition
3.7
, it is clear that
\bm{\ell}\in{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
.
∎

3.4
The space
{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
and exponentially dominated functionals

This final subsection is dedicated to the use of shuffle exponentials to dominate linear functionals. Shuffle exponentials allow for straightforward computations and it is easy to show their inclusion in
{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
, see Lemma
3.11
. Moreover, they allow for the definition of the space
{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}\subset{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
of linear functionals dominated by a shuffle exponential. This space holds interesting properties, among them,
{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
is closed under the projection operation, allowing for the inclusion of
{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
in
\mathcal{I}(\widehat{W})
, see Corollary
3.13
, making it much easier to prove that a linear functional is an Itô process.

First, we prove that
e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{b}}\in{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
:

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
\bm{b}=\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}}\bm{b}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}
with
\bm{b}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in\mathbb{R}
, then

e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{b}}={\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{b}\right)}^{-1}\in{\mathcal{A}_{h}}.

Proof.

First, the equality is ensured by Proposition
2.3
, so it suffices to prove
{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{b}\right)}^{-1}\in{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
. For simplicity, we provide a proof for the case where
d=2
, the extension to the general case follows along the same lines. For a word
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V
, we recall the notations
n({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})
and
x({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})
that denote the length of
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}
and the number of
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}
in
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}
respectively. We also recall that
h_{T}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})
depends on
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}
only through
n({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})
and
x({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})
, meaning that for any
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\in V
,
n({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})=n({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}})
and
x({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})=x({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}})
imply
h_{T}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})=h_{T}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}})
. Therefore, assuming
\bm{b}=a{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+b{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}
and
\bm{\ell}={\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-(a{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+b{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}})\right)}^{-1}
, we can decompose
\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{T}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
as:

\displaystyle\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{T}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\left|\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\right|h_{T}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}})

(3.35)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\left|a\right|^{k}\left|b\right|^{n-k}h_{T}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}^{\otimes k}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}^{\otimes n-k})

(3.36)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{k+m}{k}\left|a\right|^{k}\left|b\right|^{m}h_{T}({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}^{\otimes k}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}^{\otimes m})

(3.37)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(k+m)!}{k!~m!}\left|a\right|^{k}\left|b\right|^{m}\frac{C}{({k+m+1})^{\frac{1}{4}}}{\frac{(2T)^{k+\frac{m}{2}}}{\sqrt{(2k+m-1)!}}}

(3.38)

\displaystyle=C\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left|2Ta\right|^{k}}{k!}\frac{\left|\sqrt{2T}b\right|^{m}}{\sqrt{m!}}({k+m+1})^{-\frac{1}{4}}\sqrt{\frac{(k+m)!^{2}}{m!(2k+m-1)!}}.

(3.39)

After remarking that
({k+m+1})^{-\frac{1}{4}}\leq 1
for all
k,m\in\mathbb{N}
, we can shift our attention to the square root on the right-hand side:

\displaystyle\frac{(k+m)!^{2}}{m!(2k+m-1)!}

\displaystyle=\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{k}(m+j)}{\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}(k+m+j)}\leq k+m+1\leq(k+1)(m+1).

(3.40)

This gives

\displaystyle\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{T}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left(\sqrt{\frac{m+1}{m!}}\left|\sqrt{2T}b\right|^{m}\right)\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\sqrt{k+1}}{k!}\left|2Ta\right|^{k}\right).

(3.41)

Now, thanks to Stirling’s formula,

\frac{\sqrt{k+1}}{k!}\sim\left(\frac{e}{k}\right)^{k}\quad\text{and}\quad\sqrt{\frac{m+1}{m!}}\sim m^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(\frac{e}{m}\right)^{\frac{m}{2}}

so it is clear that both
\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{\sqrt{k+1}}{k!}x^{k}
and
\sum_{m\geq 0}\sqrt{\frac{m+1}{m!}}y^{m}
have an infinite radius of convergence, hence proving

\left|\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\right|_{T}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}<\infty.

∎

Naturally, we will define the set
{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
of all linear functionals that are
exponentially dominated
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}:=\left\{\bm{\ell}:\text{there exists $C\in\mathbb{R}_{+}$ such that }\bm{\ell}\preceq Ce^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}C\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}\right\}.

(3.42)

In dimension 1,
{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
represents the power series that have a factorial decay. Obviously
{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}\subset{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
thanks to Lemma
3.11
.

Proposition 3.12
.

{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
is closed under linear operations, shuffle product, concatenation, resolvent and projection. That is, let
\bm{\ell},\bm{\varphi}\in{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
, then

(
i
)

\alpha\bm{\ell}+\beta\bm{\varphi}\in{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
for
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,

(
ii
)

\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}}\in{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
for all
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\in V
,

(
iii
)

\bm{\ell}\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{\varphi}\in{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
,

(
iv
)

\bm{\ell}\bm{\varphi}\in{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
,

(
v
)

If in addition
\bm{\ell}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}\neq 1
,
{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{\ell}\right)}^{-1}\in{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
.

Proof.

To simplify notations, let us define
\bm{b}:=\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in A_{d}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}
. Moreover, let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
and
C_{\bm{\ell}},C_{\bm{\varphi}}>1
, and let
\bm{\ell},\bm{\varphi}\in{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
be such that
\bm{\ell}\preceq C_{\bm{\ell}}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}C_{\bm{\ell}}\bm{b}}
and
\bm{\varphi}\preceq C_{\bm{\varphi}}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}C_{\bm{\varphi}}\bm{b}}
.

(i)

Clearly, one has
\alpha\bm{\ell}+\beta\bm{\varphi}\preceq(|\alpha|C_{\bm{\ell}}+|\beta|C_{\bm{\varphi}})e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}(C_{\bm{\ell}}\vee C_{\bm{\varphi}})\bm{b}}
,

(ii)

Using Proposition
2.3
, one also has
\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}}\preceq(C_{\bm{\ell}})^{n+1}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}C_{\bm{\ell}}\bm{b}}
for
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\in V_{n}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

(iii)

Using
[
17
, Lemma 2.10]
on the shuffle product of shuffle exponentials, one can deduce

\bm{\ell}\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\bm{\varphi}\preceq C_{\bm{\ell}}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}C_{\bm{\ell}}\bm{b}}\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}C_{\bm{\varphi}}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}C_{\bm{\varphi}}\bm{b}}\preceq(C_{\bm{\ell}}C_{\bm{\varphi}})e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}(C_{\bm{\ell}}+C_{\bm{\varphi}})\bm{b}},

(iv)

Seeing that the first (and last) terms in the shuffle product are simple concatenations of the two functionals, one can conveniently dominate a concatenation by a shuffle product, i.e.

\bm{\ell}\bm{\varphi}\preceq\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\left|\bm{\varphi}\right|\preceq\left|\bm{\ell}\right|\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\left|\bm{\varphi}\right|\preceq(C_{\bm{\ell}}C_{\bm{\varphi}})e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}(C_{\bm{\ell}}+C_{\bm{\varphi}})\bm{b}},

where
\left|\bm{\ell}\right|:=\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V}\left|\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\right|{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}
,

(v)

We can first assume
\bm{\ell}^{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}=0
, as
{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{\ell}\right)}^{-1}=\frac{1}{1-\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}}}{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\frac{1}{1-\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}}}\left(\bm{\ell}-\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}\right)\right)}^{-1}
, and
C_{\bm{\ell}},C_{\bm{\varphi}}>1
without loss of generality. It implies
\bm{\ell}\preceq C_{\bm{\ell}}(e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}C_{\bm{\ell}}\bm{b}}-{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}})=(C_{\bm{\ell}})^{2}\bm{b}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}C_{\bm{\ell}}\bm{b}}
. Moreover, by Lemma
3.10
,

{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{\ell}\right)}^{-1}\preceq{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-(C_{\bm{\ell}})^{2}\bm{b}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}C_{\bm{\ell}}\bm{b}}\right)}^{-1}.

There thus only remains to make sure that, for any
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\alpha\bm{b}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\beta\bm{b}}\right)}^{-1}\in{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
. For this purpose, recalling that
{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\beta\bm{b}\right)}^{-1}({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\beta\bm{b})={\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}
, we have that

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-(\alpha+\beta)\bm{b}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\alpha\bm{b}{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\beta\bm{b}\right)}^{-1}\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\beta\bm{b}\right)-\beta\bm{b}

(3.43)

\displaystyle=\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\alpha\bm{b}{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\beta\bm{b}\right)}^{-1}\right)\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\beta\bm{b}\right),

(3.44)

which directly implies from Proposition
2.3
that

\displaystyle{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\alpha\bm{b}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\beta\bm{b}}\right)}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\beta\bm{b}\right){\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-(\alpha+\beta)\bm{b}\right)}^{-1}=\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\beta\bm{b}\right)e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}(\alpha+\beta)\bm{b}}.

(3.45)

Finally applying
(iv)
, we clearly see that the
rhs
is dominated, ending the proof.

∎

Corollary 3.13
.

Let
\bm{p},\bm{q}\in{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
,
\bm{q}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}}\neq 1
, then the unique solution
\bm{\ell}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
to the linear equation

\bm{\ell}=\bm{p}+\bm{\ell}\bm{q}

belongs to
{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
. Moreover, it also belongs to
\mathcal{I}(\widehat{W})
.

Proof.

First, applying Proposition
3.12

(iv)
-
(v)
, we obtain
\bm{\ell}=\bm{p}{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{q}\right)}^{-1}\in{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
. Then, using Proposition
3.6

(ii)
, together with Proposition
3.12

(ii)
, it follows that
{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}\subset\mathcal{I}(\widehat{W})
.
∎

Corollary
3.13
essentially implies that, provided
\bm{p}
and
\bm{q}
are exponentially dominated, an easily verifiable condition in practice,

X_{t}:=\left\langle\bm{p}{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{q}\right)}^{-1},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle

is well-defined and is an Itô process as in (
3.12
).

4
Signature representations of path-dependent processes

In this section, we provide our main signature representation formulae for linear path-dependent equations. We consider three categories:

•

Linear Volterra equations: exact, time-independent representations for smooth enough kernels, and an approximation result for more general kernels, in Section
4.1
,

•

Certain linear delay equations: exact, time-independent representations for a weighted sum of exponential kernels, in Section
4.2
,

•

Gaussian Volterra processes: exact, time-dependent representations for possibly singular kernels, including the Riemann-Liouville fractional Brownian motion for a Hurst index
H\in(0,1)
, in Section
4.3

In the sequel, we will consider the
2
-dimensional semimartingale
\widehat{W}_{t}=(t,W_{t})
, where
W
is a one-dimensional Brownian motion, we will discuss extensions for a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion in Remarks
4.6
and
4.8
.

4.1
Linear Volterra equations

The first class we consider is linear Volterra equations of the form

Y_{t}=y+\int_{0}^{t}K_{1}(t-s)\left(a_{1}+b_{1}Y_{s}\right)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}K_{2}(t-s)\left(a_{2}+b_{2}Y_{s}\right)\mathrm{d}W_{s},

(4.1)

for some real coefficients
y,a_{1},b_{1},a_{2},b_{2}
and locally square-integrable kernels
K_{1},K_{2}
. Since the drift and volatility coefficients are linear in
Y
and hence Lipschitz continuous, the stochastic integral equation (
4.1
) admits a unique strong solution
Y
, see for instance
[
4
, Theorem 3.3]
.

In our next theorem, we provide an explicit infinite linear representation for the solution
Y_{t}=\left\langle\bm{\ell},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle
in terms of the signature elements of
\widehat{W}
and time-independent coefficients
\bm{\ell}
. For this, we will need the following structure on the kernels
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
:

\displaystyle K_{1}(u)=\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{-xu}\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}x)\quad\text{ and }\quad K_{2}(u)=\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{-xu}\mu_{2}(\mathrm{d}x),

(4.2)

for finite measures
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
and such that

\int_{[0,\infty)}x^{n}\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}x)+\int_{[0,\infty)}x^{n}\mu_{2}(\mathrm{d}x)<M^{n},\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

(4.3)

for some constant
M>0
.

In particular, this implies that
K_{1},K_{2}
are infinitely continuously differentiable on
[0,T]
. For such smooth coefficients
K_{1},K_{2}
the solution
Y
is even a semimartingale.

Example 4.1
.

Let us consider
\mu(\mathrm{d}x)=f(x)\mathrm{d}x
for a positive bounded measurable function
f
with compact support. It is easy to see that for such
\mu
, the assumptions (
4.2
)-(
4.3
) are satisfied. Another example is the case
\mu(\mathrm{d}x)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}w_{k}^{n}\delta_{x_{k}^{n}}(\mathrm{d}x)
and
K(u)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}w_{k}^{n}e^{-x_{k}^{n}u}
for
w_{k}^{n},x_{k}^{n}\in\mathbb{R}
.

\blacksquare

Before we state the theorem, we need to clarify how one can make sense of
\int_{[0,\infty)}\bm{\ell}(x)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)
when
\bm{\ell}:x\mapsto\bm{\ell}(x)
is a function taking values in
T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

Definition 4.1

(Integral of parameterized linear functionals)
.

Fix a Borel measure
\mu
on
[0,\infty)
. We define
L^{1}(T((\mathbb{R}^{d})),\mu)
to be the set of all the weakly integrable maps from
\left([0,\infty),\mu\right)
to the locally convex topological vector space
T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, i.e.

L^{1}(T((\mathbb{R}^{d})),\mu):=\left\{\bm{\ell}:[0,\infty)\longrightarrow T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))\text{ s.t. }\int_{[0,\infty)}\left|\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}(x)\right|\mu(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty,\text{ for all }{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V\right\}.

Furthermore, we also define

L^{1}(\mathcal{A},\mu):=\left\{\bm{\ell}\in L^{1}(T((\mathbb{R}^{d})),\mu):\int_{[0,\infty)}\bm{\ell}(x)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)\in\mathcal{A}\right\}.

Example 4.2
.

Take
\bm{\ell}(x)=e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}-x{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}
and
\mu(\mathrm{d}x)=e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}\mathrm{d}x
, then
\bm{\ell}\in L^{1}(T((\mathbb{R}^{d})),\mu)
and thus
\int_{[0,\infty)}\bm{\ell}(x)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)
is a well-defined element of
T((\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Furthermore we can check that
\int_{[0,\infty)}\bm{\ell}(x)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)\in\mathcal{A}
, showing that
\bm{\ell}\in L^{1}(\mathcal{A},\mu)
. Now fixing
\bm{\ell}(x)=e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}-x{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}
, for a general measure
\mu
, we can easily see that
\bm{\ell}\in L^{1}(\mathcal{A},\mu)
if and only if
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{[0,\infty)}\frac{x^{n}}{n!}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty
. This partially explains why the assumption (
4.3
) arises.

\blacksquare

Theorem 4.2
.

Fix
K_{1},K_{2}
satisfying (
4.2
)-(
4.3
). The solution
Y
to the linear Volterra equation (
4.1
) admits the time-independent signature representation

\displaystyle Y_{t}=\left\langle\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{VOL}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle\text{ for every $t\leq T$, a.s., with }\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{VOL}}=\bm{p}^{\textnormal{VOL}}{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{q}^{\textnormal{VOL}}\right)}^{-1}

(4.4)

and

\displaystyle\bm{p}^{\textnormal{VOL}}:

\displaystyle=a_{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}-x{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}x)+a_{2}\left({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}-\tfrac{1}{2}b_{2}K_{2}(0){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}\right)\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}-x{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}\mu_{2}(\mathrm{d}x)+y{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}},

(4.5)

\displaystyle\bm{q}^{\textnormal{VOL}}:

\displaystyle=b_{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}-x{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}\mu_{1}({\mathrm{d}x})+b_{2}\left({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}-\tfrac{1}{2}b_{2}K_{2}(0){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}\right)\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}-x{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}\mu_{2}(\mathrm{d}x),

(4.6)

and are such that
\bm{p}^{\textnormal{VOL}},\bm{q}^{\textnormal{VOL}},\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{VOL}}\in{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
. In particular, they all belong to
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{I}(\widehat{W})
.

Proof.

The proof is given in Section
6
.
∎

For
b_{2}=0
and
K_{1}=K_{2}\equiv 1
, one recovers from the representation in Theorem
4.2
a time-independent representation for the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process as shown in the following example. For a time-dependent representation of the same process, we refer to Example
4.10
below.

Example 4.3

(Ornstein-Uhlenbeck)
.

Set
b_{2}=0
, and
\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}x)=\mu_{2}(\mathrm{d}x)=\delta_{0}(\mathrm{d}x)
, where
\delta_{0}
is the Dirac mass at
0
. In this case,
K_{1}(u)=K_{2}(u)=1
and equation (
4.1
) reads

\mathrm{d}Y_{t}=(a_{1}+b_{1}Y_{t})\mathrm{d}t+a_{2}\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad Y_{0}=y\in\mathbb{R}.

meaning that
Y
is an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process. Moreover, in this case,
\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}-x{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}x)=\\
\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}-x{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}\mu_{2}(\mathrm{d}x)={\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}
. So that

\bm{q}^{\textnormal{VOL}}=b_{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}},\quad\bm{p}^{\textnormal{VOL}}=y{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}+a_{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+a_{2}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}.

An application of Theorem
4.2
yields that the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process
Y
can be written as

Y_{t}=\left\langle\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{OU}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle

with

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{OU}}

\displaystyle=\left(y{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}+a_{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+a_{2}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}\right){\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-b_{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}\right)}^{-1}=\left(y{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}+a_{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+a_{2}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}\right)e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}b_{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}},

(4.7)

where the second equality follows from Proposition
2.3
.

\blacksquare

Furthermore, for
a_{1}=a_{2}=0
and
K_{1}=K_{2}\equiv 1
, one recovers from the representation in Theorem
4.2
the explicit solution of the geometric Brownian motion:

Example 4.4

(Geometric Brownian motion)
.

Take
a_{1}=a_{2}=0
, and
\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}x)=\mu_{2}(\mathrm{d}x)=\delta_{0}(\mathrm{d}x)
, where
\delta_{0}
is the Dirac mass at
0
. In this case,
K_{1}(u)=K_{2}(u)=1
and
\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}-x{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}-x{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}\mu_{2}(\mathrm{d}x)={\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}
.

Therefore
\bm{q}^{\textnormal{VOL}}=\left(b_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}b_{2}^{2}\right){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+b_{2}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}
,
\bm{p}^{\textnormal{VOL}}=y{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}
and thus, using Proposition
2.3
,

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{VOL}}

\displaystyle=y{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\left(b_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}b_{2}^{2}\right){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}-b_{2}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}\right)}^{-1}=ye^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\left(b_{1}-\frac{1}{2}b_{2}^{2}\right){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+b_{2}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}}.

(4.8)

It follows from the shuffle property
3.1
and the definition of the shuffle exponential in (
2.10
), that

\displaystyle Y_{t}

\displaystyle=y\left\langle e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}\left(b_{1}-\frac{1}{2}b_{2}^{2}\right){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+b_{2}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle=ye^{\left\langle\left(b_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}b_{2}^{2}\right){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+b_{2}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle}=ye^{\left(b_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}b_{2}^{2}\right)t+b_{2}W_{t}},

(4.9)

which is the geometric Brownian motion, solution to

\frac{\mathrm{d}Y_{t}}{Y_{t}}=b_{1}\mathrm{d}t+b_{2}\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad Y_{0}=y\in\mathbb{R}.

\blacksquare

Upon initial inspection, our assumptions regarding
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
may appear limiting. However, the stability results of stochastic Volterra equations allow us to approximate solutions for broader classes of kernels. For example, we can approximate solutions for the singular fractional kernel
K(t)=t^{H-1/2}
with
H\in(0,1/2)
using linear combinations of the signature elements, as demonstrated in the following corollary.

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
K_{1},K_{2}
be locally square-integrable kernels and denote by
Y
the solution of (
4.1
). For
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
i=1,2
, let
\mu_{i}^{n}(\mathrm{d}x)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}w_{k}^{n,i}\delta_{x_{k}^{n,i}}(\mathrm{d}x)
for some
w_{k}^{n,i},x_{k}^{n,i}\in\mathbb{R}
, and set
K_{1}^{n}
and
K_{2}^{n}
as in (
4.2
) and
Y_{t}^{n}=\left\langle\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{VOL}}_{n},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle
as in (
4.4
) both with
\mu_{1}^{n}
and
\mu_{2}^{n}
instead of
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
. Assume that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\left|K_{1}^{n}(s)-K_{1}(s)\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{T}\left|K_{2}^{n}(s)-K_{2}(s)\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}s\to 0,\quad\text{ as }n\to\infty.

(4.10)

Then,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|Y_{t}-\left\langle\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{VOL}}_{n},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle\right|^{p}\right]\to 0,\quad\text{ as }n\to\infty,\quad p\in\mathbb{N}.

(4.11)

Proof.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows from Example
4.1
that
\mu_{1}^{n}
and
\mu_{2}^{n}
satisfy the assumptions (
4.3
). Hence, an application of Theorem
4.2
yields that
Y^{n}_{t}=\left\langle\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{VOL}}_{n},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle
solves the stochastic Volterra equation

Y^{n}_{t}=y+\int_{0}^{t}K^{n}_{1}(t-s)\left(a_{1}+b_{1}Y_{s}\right)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}K^{n}_{2}(t-s)\left(a_{2}+b_{2}Y_{s}\right)\mathrm{d}W_{s}.

(4.12)

Routine applications of Jensen’s and Grownall’s convolution inequalities, see for instance the proof of
[
4
, Lemma 2.4]
and
[
28
, Lemma 9.8.2]
, yield that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|Y_{t}-Y^{n}_{t}\right|^{p}\right]\leq C_{p}\left(\left(\int_{0}^{T}\left|K_{1}^{n}(s)-K_{1}(s)\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}+\left(\int_{0}^{T}\left|K_{2}^{n}(s)-K_{2}(s)\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}\right),\quad p\in\mathbb{N},

(4.13)

for some
C_{p}>0
, which ends the proof.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

Different choices exist in the literature on Volterra processes for the parameters
w_{i}^{k},x_{i}^{k}
to ensure the convergence of the weighted sum of exponentials towards the initial kernels in (
4.10
) whenever
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
are completely monotone functions on
(0,\infty)
, i.e. infinitely differentiable on
(0,\infty)
such that
(-1)^{n}K^{(n)}\geq 0
. For instance, for a Hurst index
H\in(0,1/2)
, the singular fractional kernel
t^{H-1/2}
is completely monotone and admits the following representation (following Berstein’s theorem) as a Laplace transform:

\frac{t^{H-\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(H+\frac{1}{2})}=\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{-xt}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)\quad\text{ and }\quad\mu(dx)=\frac{x^{-H-\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(H+\frac{1}{2})\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-H)}\mathrm{d}x.

(4.14)

For
n\geq 1
and
r_{n}>1
, such that

\displaystyle r_{n}\downarrow 1\quad\text{ and }\quad n\ln r_{n}\to\infty,\quad\text{ as }n\to\infty,

(4.15)

with the following parametrization for the weights and the mean reversions

\displaystyle w_{k}^{n}=\frac{(r_{n}^{1-\alpha}-1)r_{n}^{(\alpha-1)(1+\frac{n}{2})}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(2-\alpha)}r_{n}^{(1-\alpha)k}\quad\text{ and }\quad x_{k}^{n}=\frac{1-\alpha}{2-\alpha}\frac{r_{n}^{2-\alpha}-1}{r_{n}^{1-\alpha}-1}r_{n}^{k-1-\frac{n}{2}},\quad\text{ for }k=1,\ldots,n,

(4.16)

where
\alpha:=H+1/2
for some
H\in(0,1/2)
, one can obtain the convergence of
\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{k}^{n}e^{-x_{k}^{n}t}
towards the fractional kernel in the sense of (
4.10
), see
[
1
,
2
]
for more details.

\blacksquare

Remark 4.6
.

Theorem
4.2
can be readily extended to the case where the Volterra equation is driven by a
(d-1)
-dimensional Brownian motion
W
of the form

Y_{t}=y+\int_{0}^{t}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}(t-s)\left(a_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+b_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}Y_{s}\right)\mathrm{d}s+\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},\dots,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{d}}}\right\}}\int_{0}^{t}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}(t-s)\left(a_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}+b_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}Y_{s}\right)\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}.

(4.17)

Here each
K_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}
is of the form
K_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}(u)=\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{-xu}\mu_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}(\mathrm{d}x)
with a finite measure
\mu_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}
satisfying (
4.3
) with the same constant
M
, for
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}},\dots,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{d}}}\right\}
. Then, similar to Theorem
4.2
, we obtain the representation

\displaystyle Y_{t}=\left\langle\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{VOL}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle,\quad\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{VOL}}=\bm{p}^{\textnormal{VOL}}{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{q}^{\textnormal{VOL}}\right)}^{-1},

(4.18)

while
\bm{p}^{\textnormal{VOL}}
and
\bm{q}^{\textnormal{VOL}}
are updated to:

\displaystyle\bm{p}^{\textnormal{VOL}}:

\displaystyle=\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}},\dots,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{d}}}\right\}}a_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}-x{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}\mu_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}(\mathrm{d}x)-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},\dots,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{d}}}\right\}}a_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}b_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}(0){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}-x{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}\mu_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}(\mathrm{d}x)+y{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}},

(4.19)

\displaystyle\bm{q}^{\textnormal{VOL}}:

\displaystyle=\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}},\dots,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{d}}}\right\}}b_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}-x{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}\mu_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}(\mathrm{d}x)-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},\dots,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{d}}}\right\}}b_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}^{2}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}(0){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}-x{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}\mu_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}(\mathrm{d}x).

(4.20)

\blacksquare

4.2
Linear delay equations

A second equation that we consider is a delay equation with linear delays both in the drift and in the volatility parts. To keep notations simple and highlight the main ideas, we restrict to weighted sum of exponential kernels:

\displaystyle dZ_{t}

\displaystyle=\left(a_{1}+b_{1}Z_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}K_{1}(t-s)Z_{s}\mathrm{d}s\right)\mathrm{d}t+\left(a_{2}+b_{2}Z_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}K_{2}(t-s)Z_{s}\mathrm{d}s\right)\mathrm{d}W_{t},

(4.21)

\displaystyle Z_{0}

\displaystyle=z\in\mathbb{R},

(4.22)

where

K_{i}(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{n_{i}}w_{i}^{k}e^{x_{i}^{k}t},

with
a_{i},b_{i},w_{i}^{k},x_{i}^{k}\in\mathbb{R},n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
for
i=1,2
. Again, it is straightforward to obtain the existence and uniqueness of a strong solution
Z
since the coefficients are linear. We stress that
Z
cannot be written as a Volterra equation in the sense of (
4.1
) unless
K_{2}
is zero.

Theorem 4.4
.

The solution
Z
to the linear delay equation (
4.21
) admits the time-independent signature representation

\displaystyle Z_{t}=\left\langle\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{DE}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle\text{ for every $t\leq T$, a.s., with }\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{DE}}=\bm{p}^{\textnormal{DE}}{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{q}^{\textnormal{DE}}\right)}^{-1},

(4.23)

and

\displaystyle\bm{p}^{\textnormal{DE}}

\displaystyle:=\left(a_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}b_{2}a_{2}\right){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+a_{2}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}+z{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}},

(4.24)

\displaystyle\bm{q}^{\textnormal{DE}}

\displaystyle:=\left(b_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}b_{2}^{2}\right){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+b_{2}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}+\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n_{1}}w_{1}^{k}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}x_{1}^{k}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}-\frac{1}{2}b_{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{2}}w_{2}^{k}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}x_{2}^{k}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}\right){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n_{2}}w_{2}^{k}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}x_{2}^{k}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}\right){{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},

(4.25)

and are such that
\bm{p}^{\textnormal{DE}},\bm{q}^{\textnormal{DE}},\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{DE}}\in{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
. In particular, they all belong to
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{I}(\widehat{W})
.

Proof.

The proof is given in Section
7
.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

For more general kernels
K_{1},K_{2}
a similar stability argument as in Corollary
4.3
allows to approximate the solution to the delay equation by linear combination of the signatures.

\blacksquare

Remark 4.8
.

Similar to Remark
4.6
, Theorem
4.4
can also be extended to the case where the Delayed equation is driven by a (
d-1
)-dimensional Brownian motion
W
of the form:

\displaystyle dZ_{t}

\displaystyle=\left(a_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+b_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}Z_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}(t-s)Z_{s}\mathrm{d}s\right)\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},\dots,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{d}}}\right\}}\left(a_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}+b_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}Z_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}K_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}(t-s)Z_{s}\mathrm{d}s\right)\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}},

(4.26)

\displaystyle Z_{0}

\displaystyle=z\in\mathbb{R},

(4.27)

where

K_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{n_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}w_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}^{k}e^{x_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}^{k}t}.

Then similar to Theorem
4.4
, we have the representation:

\displaystyle Z_{t}=\left\langle\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{DE}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle,\quad\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{DE}}=\bm{p}^{\textnormal{DE}}{\left({\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-\bm{q}^{\textnormal{DE}}\right)}^{-1},

(4.28)

while
\bm{p}^{\textnormal{DE}}
and
\bm{q}^{\textnormal{DE}}
are updated to:

\displaystyle\bm{p}^{\textnormal{DE}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}},\dots,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{d}}}\right\}}a_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},\dots,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{d}}}\right\}}a_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}b_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+z{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}},

(4.29)

\displaystyle\bm{q}^{\textnormal{DE}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}},\dots,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{d}}}\right\}}b_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},\dots,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{d}}}\right\}}b_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}^{2}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}},\dots,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{d}}}\right\}}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}}w_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}^{k}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}x_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}^{k}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\in\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},\dots,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{d}}}\right\}}b_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}}w_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}^{k}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}x_{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{j}}}^{k}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}.

(4.30)

\blacksquare

4.3
Gaussian Volterra processes

The final class that we consider are continuous Gaussian Volterra processes of the form

\displaystyle X_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}K(t-s)\mathrm{d}W_{s},

(4.31)

for a locally square-integrable deterministic kernel
K
that is infinitely differentiable on
(0,T]
and satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left|K^{(n)}(t)\right|\frac{t^{n+\frac{1}{2}}}{n!\sqrt{2n+1}}<\infty\quad\text{ and }\quad\int_{0}^{t}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left|K^{(n)}(t)\right|\frac{s^{n}}{n!}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}s<\infty,\quad t\in(0,T],\\
&\int_{0}^{T}\big(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left|K^{(n)}(t)\right|\frac{t^{n+\frac{1}{2}}}{n!\sqrt{2n+1}}\big)\mathrm{d}t<\infty,\end{cases}

(4.32)

where
K^{(n)}
denotes the
n
-th derivative on
(0,T]
.

We note that here, and in contrast with Theorem
4.2
,
K
can have a singularity at
0
, as shown in Example
4.11
for the Riemann-Liouville fractional Brownian motion. For such kernels, Corollary
4.3
already provides an approximation result by infinite linear combinations of signature elements of
\widehat{W}
with time-independent coefficients
\bm{\ell}
. More interestingly, the following theorem shows that exact infinite linear representation of
X
are possible but with time-dependent coefficients
\bm{\ell}_{t}
. Time-dependent representations seemed crucial for us to obtain exact representations for singular kernels.

Theorem 4.5
.

Fix
K:(0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying (
4.32
), the Gaussian Volterra process
X
defined in (
4.31
) admits a time-dependent signature representation

X_{t}=\left\langle\bm{\ell}_{t}^{\textnormal{GV}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle,\quad a.s.

for every
t\leq T
, with
\bm{\ell}_{t}^{\textnormal{GV}}
given by

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}_{t}^{\textnormal{GV}}=\begin{cases}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}K^{(n)}(t)(-1)^{n}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}^{\otimes n}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}&\text{if }t\in(0,T],\\
0&\text{else.}\end{cases}

(4.33)

Proof.

The proof is given in Section
8
.
∎

Remark 4.9
.

Unlike the representations in Theorems
4.2
and
4.4
, here
\left\langle\bm{\ell}_{t}^{\textnormal{GV}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle
is a modification of
X_{t}
and it is not necessarily indistinguishable from the process
X
.

\blacksquare

The following example shows that the exponential kernel
K(t)=e^{-\kappa t}
for
\kappa>0
satisfies the assumption of Theorem
4.5
. This provides a time-dependent representation of the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process, recall Example
4.3
for the time-independent representation.

Example 4.10

(Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process)
.

Let

X_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\kappa(t-s)}\mathrm{d}W_{s}=\int_{0}^{t}K(t-s)\mathrm{d}W_{s}

with
K(t)=e^{-\kappa t}
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
. We first check that
K
satisfies (
4.32
). Clearly
K
is infinitely differentiable on
[0,T]
such that
\left|K^{(n)}(t)\right|=\left|\kappa\right|^{n}K(t)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left|K^{(n)}(t)\right|s^{n}}{n!}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{t}\left(K(t)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\left|\kappa\right|s)^{n}}{n!}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}s=K(t)^{2}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\left|\kappa\right|s}\mathrm{d}s<\infty.

(4.34)

Moreover

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left|K^{(n)}(t)\right|}{n!\sqrt{2n+1}}t^{n+\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{t}e^{-\kappa t}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\left|\kappa\right|t)^{n}}{n!}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2n+1}}\leq\sqrt{T}e^{\left|\kappa\right|T}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\left|\kappa\right|t)^{n}}{n!}=\sqrt{T}e^{2\left|\kappa\right|T}<\infty.

Thus

\int_{0}^{T}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left|K^{(n)}(t)\right|}{n!\sqrt{2n+1}}t^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\right)\mathrm{d}t\leq T^{\frac{3}{2}}e^{2\left|\kappa\right|T}<\infty.

Finally, Theorem
4.5
yields the signature representation
X_{t}=\left\langle\bm{\ell}_{t}^{\textnormal{OU}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle
, with

\bm{\ell}_{t}^{\textnormal{OU}}=e^{-\kappa t}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\kappa^{n}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}^{\otimes n}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}.

(4.35)

Note that we can more compactly write it as

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}_{t}^{\textnormal{OU}}=e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}-\kappa(t{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}})}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},

(4.36)

which is the algebraic translation of the probabilistic expression

X_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\kappa(t-s)}\mathrm{d}W_{s}.

\blacksquare

The following example shows that the fractional kernel
K(t)=t^{H-1/2}
for
H\in(0,1)
, with singularity at
0
for
H<1/2
, satisfies the assumption of Theorem
4.5
. This provides a representation of the Riemann-Liouville fractional Brownian motion, and shows that infinite linear combinations of signature elements are not always semimartingales, recall Remark
3.8
.

Example 4.11

(Riemann-Liouville fractional Brownian motion)
.

Let

X_{t}=\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(H+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{H-\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{d}W_{s}=\int_{0}^{t}K(t-s)\mathrm{d}W_{s},

with
K(t)=\frac{t^{H-\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(H+\frac{1}{2})}
. We first check that
K
satisfies (
4.32
). Clearly
K
is analytical on
(0,T]
for
H\in(0,1)
:

\displaystyle K^{(n)}(t)

\displaystyle=K(t)t^{-n}\left(H-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{\underline{n}}=K(t)(-t)^{-n}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}-H\right)^{\bar{n}}

(4.37)

where
x^{\underline{n}}=\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}(x-k)
and
x^{\bar{n}}=\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}(x+k)
denote the falling and rising factorials respectively.

Furthermore, one can then remark that

\displaystyle\left|K^{(n)}(t)\right|

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}K^{(n)}(t)(-1)^{n}&\text{if $H<\frac{1}{2}$,}\\
K^{(n)}(t)(-1)^{n+1}&\text{else.}\end{cases}

(4.38)

Therefore

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left|K^{(n)}(t)\right|s^{n}}{n!}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\left(K(t)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(H-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{\underline{n}}\frac{1}{n!}\left(\frac{-s}{t}\right)^{n}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{t}K(t-s)^{2}\mathrm{d}s<\infty,

(4.39)

where the last equality comes from the Taylor expansion of
(1-x)^{H-1/2}
which converges for
\left|x\right|<1
. Moreover we have

\displaystyle\left|K^{(n)}(t)\right|

\displaystyle=K(t)\frac{\Gamma\left(n+\tfrac{1}{2}-H\right)}{\Gamma\left(\tfrac{1}{2}-H\right)}t^{-n}=O\left(\left(\tfrac{n}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}-H}(n-1)!~t^{-n}\right),\quad n>0,

(4.40)

therefore

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left|K^{(n)}(t)\right|}{n!\sqrt{2n+1}}t^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\leq Ct^{H}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-1-H}<\infty.

And

\int_{0}^{T}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left|K^{(n)}(t)\right|}{n!\sqrt{2n+1}}t^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\right)\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{CT^{H+1}}{H+1}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-1-H}<\infty.

Finally, Theorem
4.5
yields the signature representation
X_{t}=\left\langle\bm{\ell}_{t}^{\textnormal{RL}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle
, with

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}_{t}^{\textnormal{RL}}

\displaystyle=\frac{t^{H-\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(H+\frac{1}{2})}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}t^{-n}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}-H\right)^{\bar{n}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}^{\otimes n}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},

(4.41)

where
(\cdot)^{\bar{n}}
is the rising factorial.
Remarking that
\left(\tfrac{1}{2}-H\right)^{\bar{n}}=0
for all
n>0
when
H=\frac{1}{2}
gives
\bm{\ell}_{t}={{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}
, the coordinate of
W_{t}
in
\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}
. Note that we can also formally write it as

\bm{\ell}_{t}^{\textnormal{RL}}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(H+\frac{1}{2})}(t{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}})^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}H-\frac{1}{2}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},

which is the algebraic translation of the probabilistic expression

X_{t}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(H+\frac{1}{2})}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{H-\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{d}W_{s}.

\blacksquare

4.4
Numerical illustrations

In this subsection, we provide numerical implementations of the truncated form of the signature processes
X^{\leq M}
defined by

X_{t}^{\leq M}:=\left\langle\bm{\ell},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}^{\leq M}\right\rangle,\quad M\geq 0,

where
\bm{\ell}\in T((\mathbb{R}^{2}))
and
\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}^{\leq M}:[0,T]\to T^{M}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is defined in (
2.19
), i.e. its
M
first levels coincide with
\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}
and everything else is set to
0
.

4.4.1
Shifted Riemann-Liouville fractional Brownian motion

Figure
1
and Table
1
display simulations of a shifted Riemann-Liouville fractional Brownian motion
X^{\varepsilon}
together with mean-squared errors, i.e.

X_{t}^{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(H+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{t}(t+\varepsilon-s)^{H-\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{d}W_{s},

and its linear representation counterpart
\bm{\ell}_{\cdot+\varepsilon}^{\textnormal{RL}}
, following from (
4.41
), that is

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}_{t+\varepsilon}^{\textnormal{RL}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(t+\varepsilon)^{H-\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(H+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(t+\varepsilon)^{-n}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}-H\right)^{\bar{n}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}^{\otimes n}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}=\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(H+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\left((t+\varepsilon){\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\textup{{\o{}}}}-{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}\right)^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}H-\frac{1}{2}}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},

(4.42)

for several truncation orders
M\in\mathbb{N}
. The reason for shifting the kernel is to speed up the convergence as it can be quite slow for small values of
H
when
\varepsilon=0
.

H=0.9

), i.e.

, for several truncation orders

and

.

and Figure

the clear convergence of the truncated linear representation for several values of
H
. The smaller
H
is, the slower the convergence. We can also see that the fit deteriorates with the time variable
t
.

and Table

display simulations of a delay equation and its truncated representations together with mean-squared errors following Theorem
4.4
. This convergence is shown for two sets of parameters: (a) has its
b_{1}=0
and
x_{i}>0
whereas (b) has
b_{2}=0
and
x_{i}<0
.

(a)
z=0.25,a_{1}=0.25,b_{1}=0,n_{1}=1,w_{1}=-4,x_{1}=1,a_{2}=1,b_{2}=1,n_{2}=1,w_{2}=-2,x_{2}=1.5

, for several truncation orders
M
.

Recall that here since
\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{DE}}\in{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
, the inequality (
3.21
) could in principle yield a control of the strong error, i.e. for every
\epsilon>0
, there exists
M_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\left\langle\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{DE}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}^{\leq M_{\epsilon}}\right\rangle-\left\langle\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{DE}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle\right|\right]\leq\epsilon.

For related numerical illustrations with other processes, we refer to
Abi Jaber and Gérard [3]
.

4.4.3
Linear Volterra process

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we consider a linear Volterra process
Y^{n}
solution to the Volterra equation (
4.1
), where the kernel is given by a sum of
n
weighted exponentials

\displaystyle K_{n}^{\alpha,\varepsilon}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}e^{-x_{i}t}.

(4.43)

We denote by
\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{VOL}}_{n}
the exact coefficients given from the signature representation in Theorem
4.2
and we consider
\langle\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{VOL}}_{n},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}^{\leq M}\rangle
its truncated exact representation at level
M\geq 1
. The parameters
(x_{i},w_{i})_{i=1,\ldots,n}
are chosen in such a way to optimally approximates the
\varepsilon
-shifted fractional kernel

\displaystyle K^{\alpha,\varepsilon}(t):=\frac{(t+\varepsilon)^{\alpha-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{yt}\mu^{\alpha,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}y),\quad\text{with}\quad\mu^{\alpha,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}y):=\frac{y^{-\alpha}e^{-y\varepsilon}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\mathrm{d}y.

(4.44)

Given a partition
(\eta_{i})_{0\leq i\leq n}
where
0=\eta_{0}<\eta_{1}<\cdots<\eta_{n}<\infty
, we can readily compute its corresponding points
x_{i}
and weights
w_{i}

\displaystyle w_{i}

\displaystyle=\int_{\eta_{i-1}}^{\eta_{i}}\mu^{\alpha,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}y)=\varepsilon^{\alpha-1}\frac{\Gamma\left(1-\alpha;\varepsilon\,\eta_{i-1},\varepsilon\,\eta_{i}\right)}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)},

(4.45)

\displaystyle x_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{i}}\int_{\eta_{i-1}^{n}}^{\eta_{i}^{n}}y\;\mu^{\alpha,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}y)=\frac{\varepsilon^{\alpha-2}}{w_{i}}\frac{\Gamma\left(2-\alpha;\varepsilon\,\eta_{i-1},\varepsilon\,\eta_{i}\right)}{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)},

(4.46)

where
\Gamma\left(z;a,b\right)=\int_{a}^{b}t^{z-1}e^{-t}\mathrm{d}z
is the incomplete gamma function. We chose a geometric partition
\eta_{i}=(r_{n})^{i-n/2}
for some
L^{2}
-optimal
r_{n}>0
as in
[
1
,
2
,
13
]
, i.e.

\displaystyle r_{n}

\displaystyle=\arg\min_{r}||K_{n}^{\alpha,\varepsilon}-K^{\alpha,\varepsilon}||_{L^{2}(\tau,T)}

(4.47)

\displaystyle=\arg\min_{r}\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}w_{i}w_{j}\frac{e^{-(x_{i}+x_{j})\tau}-e^{-(x_{i}+x_{j})T}}{x_{i}+x_{j}}-2\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}(x_{i})^{-\alpha}e^{x_{i}\varepsilon}\frac{\Gamma\left(\alpha;(\tau+\varepsilon)\,x_{i},(T+\varepsilon)\,x_{i}\right)}{\Gamma{(\alpha)}}\right).

(4.48)

In Figure
3
, and later in Figure
4
, we compare the truncated signature representation
\langle\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{VOL}}_{n},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}^{\leq M}\rangle
of the multifactor approximation of the Volterra process
Y^{n}
with the kernel (
4.43
) to the Euler discretization of the (true) Volterra process
Y
, i.e.

Y_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}K^{\alpha,\epsilon}(t-s)((a_{1}+b_{1}Y_{s})\mathrm{d}s+((a_{2}+b_{2}Y_{s})\mathrm{d}W_{s}).

In particular, this provides a numerical illustration of the approximation result given in Corollary
4.3
. Figure
3
shows that for different values of
\alpha
, the truncated signature of the multifactor approximation with
n=10
factors yields very accurate sample paths, at least for short time horizons.

(a)
\alpha=0.2,r_{n}=2.936

(b)
\alpha=0.4,r_{n}=2.884

(c)
\alpha=0.6,r_{n}=2.831

(d)
\alpha=0.8,r_{n}=2.778

Figure 3:
Trajectories of the shifted fractional Volterra process (black) against their truncated time-independent linear multifactor approximation representation (
4.4
), i.e.
\left\langle\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{VOL}}_{n},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}^{\leq M}\right\rangle
, for several truncation orders
M
and
y=0.25,a_{1}=0.25,b_{1}=-1,a_{2}=0,b_{2}=1,\varepsilon=1/52,T=1/12,\tau=1/106,n=10
.

5
Conditional moments

As a key application of Section
4.1
and Section
4.2
, our representation formulas provide explicit expressions for the conditional moments of Volterra and delay processes, which can be efficiently computed numerically without relying on Monte Carlo simulations.

The computation of unconditional and conditional moments is a central but notoriously difficult problem for non-Markovian processes. Our approach leverages the Markovianity of the signature of
\widehat{\mathbb{W}}
to make even conditional moments straightforward to obtain. The linear Volterra process (
4.1
) is a particular case of a polynomial Volterra process studied in
[
5
]
, where unconditional moments were derived in terms of deterministic integral equations, without any treatment of conditional moments or numerical methods. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first time that explicit formulas are used to implement efficient numerics for both unconditional and conditional moments in this setting. Moreover, the delayed case (
4.21
) with nonzero
b_{2}
or
K_{2}
lies outside the polynomial Volterra family and thus cannot be recovered from
[
5
]
, further underlining the significance of our representation formulas.

Theorem
5.2
establishes the conditional moment formulas combining our representation formulas in (
4.4
) and (
4.23
) with the celebrated Fawcett formula
[
21
]
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}]=\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t},\quad t\leq T,

with

\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{t}:=e^{\otimes\left({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+\frac{1}{2}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{22}}}\right)t}.

Before stating Theorem
5.2
, we will generalize the projection defined in (
2.3
) against linear functionals of the form

\bm{\varphi}=e^{\otimes a_{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+b_{2}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{22}}}}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}\left(a_{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+a_{2}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{22}}}\right)^{\otimes m},

and show that under this framework it is the adjoint concatenation operation.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\bm{\ell}\in{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
and
\bm{\varphi}=e^{\otimes a_{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{i}}}\in\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}},\cdots,{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{d}}}\right\}}a_{i}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{ii}}}}
for some constants
a_{1},a_{2},\cdots,a_{d}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

\bm{\ell}|_{\bm{\varphi}}:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\varphi}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}}\in{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}

and

\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{\bm{\varphi}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle=\left\langle\bm{\ell},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\otimes\bm{\varphi}\right\rangle,\quad t\leq T.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we will assume
d=2
. Now, recall that for all
C>0
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}
with
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}C({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}})}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}}\preceq C^{n}e^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}C({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}})}
. This essentially means that we are only interested in the length
n
of
{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}
when dominating projections of elements of
{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
. Then, since by assumption on
\bm{\ell}
, there exists
C>1
such that
\bm{\ell}\preceq Ce^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}C({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}})}
, we can write

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}|_{\bm{\varphi}}

\displaystyle\preceq\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{k=0}^{m}\binom{m}{k}\left|a_{1}\right|^{m-k}\left|a_{2}\right|^{k}Ce^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}C({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}})}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}^{\otimes m-k}{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}}^{\otimes 2k}}

(5.1)

\displaystyle\preceq Ce^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}C({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}})}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{\left|a_{1}\right|^{m-k}}{(m-k)!}\frac{\left|a_{2}\right|^{k}}{k!}C^{m+k}

(5.2)

\displaystyle\preceq Ce^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}C({{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{1}}}+{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{2}}})}e^{\left(C\left|a_{1}\right|+C^{2}\left|a_{2}\right|\right)},

(5.3)

which clearly shows
\bm{\ell}|_{\bm{\varphi}}\in{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
. We can now decompose
\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{\bm{\varphi}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle
as follows

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{\bm{\varphi}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\varphi}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\left\langle\bm{\ell}|_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle

(5.4)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\bm{\varphi}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\in V_{m}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{uv}}}\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}

(5.5)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\in V_{m+n}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\in V_{m}}\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\bm{\varphi}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\mathds{1}_{\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{uv}}}\right\}}

(5.6)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{k}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\in V_{k}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\in V_{n}}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\in V_{k-n}}\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{u}}}\bm{\varphi}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{v}}}\mathds{1}_{\left\{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{uv}}}\right\}}

(5.7)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\in V_{k}}\bm{\ell}^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}\left(\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\otimes\bm{\varphi}\right)^{{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\mathbf{w}}}

(5.8)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\bm{\ell},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\otimes\bm{\varphi}\right\rangle,

(5.9)

thereby completing the proof.
∎

Theorem 5.2
.

Consider the representation formulas for
Y
and
Z
given by (
4.4
) and (
4.23
).
Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{T}^{m}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]=\left\langle(\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{VOL}})^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}m}|_{\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{T-t}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle,\quad\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{T}^{m}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]=\left\langle(\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{DE}})^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}m}|_{\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{T-t}},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\right\rangle,\quad t\leq T.

(5.10)

Proof.

By Theorems
4.2
and
4.4
, we have
\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{VOL}},\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{DE}}\in{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
. Proposition
3.12

(iii)
thus makes it clear that
(\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{VOL}})^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}m},(\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{DE}})^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}m}\in{\mathcal{A}_{\exp}}
. We then apply the shuffle property of Proposition
3.1
to write

Y_{T}^{m}=\left\langle(\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{VOL}})^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}m},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{T}\right\rangle,\qquad Z_{T}^{m}=\left\langle(\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{DE}})^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}m},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{T}\right\rangle.

Applying the dominated convergence theorem, we get

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\bm{\ell}^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}m},\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{T}\right\rangle\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]=\left\langle\bm{\ell}^{\mathrel{\sqcup\mkern-3.0mu\sqcup}m},\mathbb{E}\left[\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{T}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]\right\rangle,

for
\bm{\ell}\in\big\{\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{VOL}},\bm{\ell}^{\textnormal{DE}}\big\}
. Using Chen’s identity
[
12
]
, together with Fawcett’s formula
[
21
]
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{T}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]=\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\otimes\mathbb{E}\left[\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{T-t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]=\widehat{\mathbb{W}}_{t}\otimes\bm{\mathcal{E}}_{T-t}.

Finally, we apply Lemma
5.1
which completes the proof.
∎

Y

K^{\alpha,\epsilon}
, and the signature representations are computed using the multifactor approximation. This illustrates once again the usefulness of Corollary
4.3
.

) to the

, for several truncation orders

and
